#6021. Iran Is Talking Peace but Preparing for War

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Iran Is Talking Peace but Preparing for War

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Last week, a high-ranking set of Saudi, Pakistani, and Turkish officials came to Tehran, where they met an Iranian delegation led by Mohsen Rezaei. Rezaei, former commander of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), heads Iran’s Supreme National Security Council (SNSC). The SNSC is the country’s top decision-making body and brings together leaders from the IRGC and conventional military, along with representatives of the supreme leader and elected officials. In this meeting, Rezaei was joined by President Masoud Pezeshkian and Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi.

That these officials—representing Iran’s top security body, its president, and its foreign minister—all participated in the meeting suggests a degree of consensus across Iran’s political spectrum. In the past, many Iranian diplomatic efforts had been the sole prerogative of the foreign ministry, without buy-in from military and security institutions, which made their implementation difficult. This meeting, on the other hand, demonstrates that, across different centers of power, Iran’s political and military establishment is serious about diplomacy.

Last week, a high-ranking set of Saudi, Pakistani, and Turkish officials came to Tehran, where they met an Iranian delegation led by Mohsen Rezaei. Rezaei, former commander of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), heads Iran’s Supreme National Security Council (SNSC). The SNSC is the country’s top decision-making body and brings together leaders from the IRGC and conventional military, along with representatives of the supreme leader and elected officials. In this meeting, Rezaei was joined by President Masoud Pezeshkian and Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi.

That these officials—representing Iran’s top security body, its president, and its foreign minister—all participated in the meeting suggests a degree of consensus across Iran’s political spectrum. In the past, many Iranian diplomatic efforts had been the sole prerogative of the foreign ministry, without buy-in from military and security institutions, which made their implementation difficult. This meeting, on the other hand, demonstrates that, across different centers of power, Iran’s political and military establishment is serious about diplomacy.

The seniority of the Iranians’ interlocutors also underscores the importance of these talks. Pakistan was represented by Field Marshal Asim Munir, the country’s de facto most influential political figure. Saudi Arabia sent Defense Minister Khalid bin Salman, the younger brother and confidant of Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman, while Turkey sent intelligence chief Ibrahim Kalin, a longtime close advisor to President Recep Tayyip Erdogan with extensive experience in regional diplomacy.

Saudi Arabia, Turkey, and Pakistan are also members of the Mecca defense pact, suggesting that Iran wants to play a greater role in the regional security architecture rather than acting outside of it. This is important, as Saudi Arabia in particular faces both continued attacks from Iran’s Houthi allies and risks becoming the target of IRGC strikes should the war break out again.

The meeting ended with a joint statement signed by Rezaei, Salman, Munir, and Kalin in which the four committed their countries to continued dialogue and regional “peace and security.” The statement was vague but signals that Iran does not want to immediately escalate and is instead open to having a productive relationship with its neighbors, some of whom it was targeting with missile and drone strikes just months earlier. This is supported by reports that the Iranian leadership responded positively to a letter sent last month by U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance to Rezaei, in which the former stressed the need for diplomacy.

But the meeting came on the heels of another significant development in the Iranian security landscape that signals that Iran is preparing to escalate if it must: the appointment of Mehrdad Akhlaghi as defense minister. His appointment indicates a shift in the defense establishment to a focus on offensive capabilities. Akhlaghi previously led the Shahid Bagheri Industrial Group (SBIG), where he worked on developing Iran’s ballistic missiles, particularly those that use solid-fuel propellants. Compared to liquid-fuel missiles, solid-fuel missiles are easier to store, require less maintenance, and are less vulnerable to detection. As much of the U.S.-Israel strategy during the war centered on neutralizing Iran’s missiles, the importance of these operationally advantageous missiles should war break out again will increase—a consideration that may have played a role in Akhlaghi’s appointment.

After the SBIG, Akhlaghi went up the Iranian military-industrial ladder. He led the Aerospace Industries Organization, which oversees the missile program as a whole. He then headed the Defense Industries Organization, which oversees the manufacturing of all Iranian military hardware, including drones, which were also key to Iran’s military operations in the Gulf during this year’s war. His appointment marks a shift from his predecessor, Aziz Nasirzadeh, who was a former fighter pilot and came from Iran’s conventional air force, rather than institutions associated with the IRGC and its weaponry. Nasirzadeh was killed during the Feb. 28 attacks and, since then, the defense ministry had been led in the interim by Majid Ebn-e-Reza.

Akhlaghi will be tasked with rapidly rebuilding Iranian military capabilities—mainly missiles and drones—that have been degraded by U.S. and Israeli attacks. This suggests that Iran is preparing, simply put, to pummel the Gulf countries if war breaks out again.

In a recent speech, Akhlaghi stated that “if [U.S. President Donald] Trump drops a single bomb, his forces are going to leave the region horizontally (i.e., in body bags).” That would require a significant campaign against the Gulf—precisely the type of escalation that Salman was likely seeking to prevent during his time in Tehran. Akhlaghi’s influence in Iran could extend beyond the defense ministry to the SNSC, where he can participate in deliberations and vote on matters concerning national defense.

An insistence on regional diplomacy while simultaneously appointing a defense minister whose primary responsibility revolves around rebuilding the capabilities designed to wreak havoc on the Gulf might seem contradictory. But these moves reveal Iran’s broader strategy of hedging, at the core of which is a simple reality: Iran does not seem to know what is coming next.

In this state of limbo, Iran is watching international developments for clues as to what may be in store. Israeli elections, for example, have been covered extensively in Iranian media. Conservative outlets close to the supreme leader’s office, for instance, have amplified reports that Gadi Eisenkot, unlike incumbent Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, is against regime change operations in Iran, as he has recognized that the Islamic Republic cannot be overthrown militarily. Eisenkot does not fully oppose military operations but instead believes that, should Israel attack Iran, it should target specific installations, such as nuclear facilities, rather than launch an all-out war.

Such reporting shows that, before the election, there was some cautious optimism in Iranian political circles: An Eisenkot-led government would be less willing to restart the war against Iran and might revert to smaller-scale sabotage operations that were common before the wars. From Tehran’s point of view, a change in Israeli leadership could also reduce the likelihood of renewed U.S. intervention, given Iran’s belief that Israel dragged the U.S. into the war.

Though Likud emerged as the party with the most votes, Netanyahu’s coalition lost its majority, and Eisenkot has warned Netanyahu against conducting an attack in the absence of a majority—even going as far as to call on security officials to stand firm against the prime minister. Netanyahu will have a tough time forming a government, as he would need to coax members of Eisenkot’s bloc into defecting. But Eisenkot, whose party underperformed in the elections, will also not be able to form a government without the support of Arab parties, which he has previously expressed opposition to including in any coalition.

Iran is also watching the American midterm elections, which similarly provoke more questions than answers. Some Iranian media sources have relayed reports from U.S. outlets that a Democratic victory in the midterms could constrain Trump’s ability to sustain a prolonged military campaign against Iran. Yet even if Democrats win control of Congress, they will not take office until January, leaving Trump two months to act without facing the constraints of a Democrat-controlled legislature. Once his party is freed from electoral pressures and concerns over rising gas prices, he might capitalize on that window to resume military action—even as his vice president is attempting to pursue diplomacy.

This uncertainty lies at the heart of Iran’s strategic calculations. Iran wants to convince its neighbors that it can be a partner for peace, while rebuilding its capacity to wage war. Whether diplomacy succeeds—or even has a chance to succeed—will depend on several factors, including who comes to power in Israel and how Trump acts after the midterms. In the meantime, Tehran is hoping for the best but expecting the worst.

Ashkan Hashemipour is a D.Phil. candidate at the University of Oxford in modern Iranian history and politics.

#6018. The Limits of China’s Self-Sufficiency

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JOEL WUTHNOW is a Senior Research Fellow at the Institute for National Strategic Studies at the National Defense University and a co-author of China’s Quest for Military Supremacy. The views expressed here are his own.

Why the Country’s Food Insecurity Could Prevent a War Over Taiwan

When assessing the probability of a conflict in the Taiwan Strait, analysts tend to focus on a few obvious risks that China would face—namely, military defeat, economic slowdown, and restricted oil and technology imports. All of these are important, but an equally significant vulnerability is often left off the list: China’s reliance on soybean imports.

Beijing recognizes this liability. At the 20th Party Congress, in 2022, Chinese leader Xi Jinping identified “ensuring the security of food, energy resources, and critical industrial supply chains” as issues essential to national security and social stability. Food security, conspicuously listed first, is an especially sensitive issue for China’s 1.4 billion inhabitants: roughly 15 percent of the country’s population today was alive during the 1959–61 famine, which resulted in an estimated 30 million deaths.

As with technology and oil, Beijing has tried to limit its dependence on soybeans by building up stockpiles and diversifying its sources. But because soybeans are a fragile foodstuff, there is only so much China can do. Beijing can support a war effort, therefore, for only as long as the Chinese public could put the People’s Liberation Army’s success above its nutritional needs. And the key for deterring Chinese leaders from starting a conflict is to convince them that a domestic crisis would arrive sooner than any battlefield victory.

Disruption to critical imports is one of the most powerful deterrents to China’s use of force, and it’s a threat that the Chinese government has spent decades working to counter. The specter of a foreign blockade in the Strait of Malacca, through which most of Chinese oil imports pass, has worried Chinese leaders since at least 2003, when Chinese leader Hu Jintao warned about the risk of major powers’ interference creating a “Malacca dilemma” for China’s energy security. Beijing, however, has stockpiled massive reserves of oil, which it could complement in a crisis by increasing imports of Russian oil and, as Moscow has done, building up a sanctions-evading shadow fleet to transport oil from the Middle East.

Access to foreign technology is a newer but just as important concern, and China is similarly stockpiling critical components, such as chips. It is also having success in developing local alternatives. Beijing has already pulled ahead in some sectors, such as solar and battery manufacturing, or it is working to close the gap in others, as in semiconductors.

But Beijing has fewer options when it comes to self-sufficiency in food. Domestic production of staple grains—such as wheat, rice, and corn—accounts for more than 90 percent of consumption in these categories, but China remains dependent on foreign producers for other important foodstuffs, including soybeans, beef, and barley. Between 2000 and 2020, the share of China’s food supply provided by imports grew from six percent to 34 percent, and analysts predict it to increase to 41 percent by 2030. Part of this growth comes from the country’s economic development; as people become wealthier, their diets diversify, and they are no longer satisfied eating only grains. Between 1962 and 2024, meat as a percentage of the typical Chinese diet increased by a factor of 18.

This is where the central role of soybeans comes in. The legume provides a complex array of proteins in animal feed that other food sources and synthetic amino acids cannot replicate. Without it, livestock herds in China would shrink, thereby threatening domestic meat and dairy supplies. Soybean oil is also the most used cooking oil in China, making up nearly half the market. Alternatives, such as palm and rapeseed oils, are also mostly imported and face the same risks of maritime disruption, offering little relief.

China has invested a great deal in securing and, when possible, diversifying its access to foreign food sources. In 2013, a Chinese company bought Smithfield Foods, the United States’ largest pork producer. And state-owned enterprises such as COFCO International, a branch of China’s largest food and agriculture company, are buying farms and building logistical infrastructure in major agriculture-producing countries, such as Argentina and Brazil, which allows them to buy directly from farmers. Such direct investments provide a buffer against price volatility, giving Beijing more control over the volume and cost of supplies at their source. China has also steadily increased its own domestic meat production, which accounts for more than 90 percent of China’s demand. But such efforts do little to mitigate the effects of disruptions in maritime shipping—especially of soybeans, which is a supply chain that is fragile even in peacetime.

The central rub is that China has no choice but to import its soybeans. There is not enough arable land in China to both expand soybean cultivation and sustain self-reliance in staple grains. (Soybeans are also a much less efficient crop than corn or wheat in China’s arid north, making this tradeoff the economical choice.) But the countries that grow and export soybeans are almost all in the Western Hemisphere, which presents even more logistical challenges. When the trade war between the United States and China broke out in 2018, for instance, Beijing limited its U.S. soybean imports from 40 percent to about 20 percent of its supply, making up the difference with increased imports from Brazil. But Brazil’s poor infrastructure and frequent natural disasters make its harvest more susceptible to failure than the U.S. harvest. In 2024, one Brazilian state lost a quarter to a third of its soybean harvest because of flooding.

U.S. and Brazilian ports are also about as far from China as they could be. Brazil’s Santos port, for example, is more than 10,000 miles from Guangzhou, which takes 45 days to ship. Because of soybeans’ sensitivity to humidity, this distance means that ships often arrive in China with rotten cargo. For similar reasons, the legume is also difficult to stockpile. Unlike rice and wheat reserves, which Beijing claims could last for years, soybean reserves are smaller and must be replaced more frequently. Chinese stockpiles might be able to last only a few months if its imports were cut off. Although China has been experimenting with bioengineered supplements in an effort to reduce the amount of soybeans in animal feed, rising food demand is offsetting any gains. In other words, its dependence on soybean imports is only continuing to grow.

The outbreak of war between China and the United States would pose existential threats to this supply chain. In the most extreme scenario, the U.S. military could block shipments from the Western Hemisphere to China, which would take away most of the country’s soybean imports. China has no naval bases or forward forces in Latin America that could counter these efforts.

But China’s food supply chains would suffer from disruption even without a U.S. embargo or blockade. Any war in the Pacific would bring with it massive risk premiums for maritime shipment insurance that might cause shipping firms to balk. Such an effect has already been on display for shipping in the Red Sea, where Houthi missile threats have caused insurance costs to skyrocket since 2024. A war in the Pacific could be far more intense and costly because it would involve major navies, and ships could not route around the danger zone, making the long-haul journey from Brazil financially ruinous for many companies.

Beijing could, in theory, offer sovereign guarantees to offset firms’ insurance premiums. Even then, the firms transporting soybeans, most of which are owned by U.S. and allied country parties, might cease transporting products to China to avoid abetting a hostile regime, to steer clear of dangers that would put their employees at risk, and to head off possible future exposure to sanctions for doing business with the country.

Chinese analysts believe that China could circumvent some of these risks by using its own commercial fleet. Although this fleet has impressive capacity during peacetime—the Chinese shipping giant COSCO owns 468 dry bulk carriers—in wartime it would be stretched thin, facing demands to help move construction materials, industrial supplies, and foodstuffs for the People’s Liberation Army and to transport critical military materials such as coal and iron ore. Even available ships would likely lack personnel with the training needed to keep soybeans fresh during the long journey. And if ships tried to avoid the most dangerous sea-lanes, trips could take even longer, surpassing the 40 days that soybeans can be safely stored. Cargo might arrive rotten, if it arrived at all.

A war in the Pacific would not trigger an immediate food crisis in China. In the shorter term, the government could manage reduced imports by releasing supplies of soybeans, frozen pork, and other staples from state reserves. It could also reduce the minimum soybean inclusion rates for animal feed, which would slow livestock growth and cut meat output but which would be workable for a time. A few months of a managed crunch, however, is a best-case scenario. More likely is that panic buying and shipping disruptions would drain reserves faster than planned, turning a manageable shortfall into rising prices and visible shortages within months.

There is reason to believe, for instance, that Chinese stockpiles might not provide the short-term security they purport to. Over the last decade, local government warehouses meant to store emergency food supplies have been found empty or filled with rotten supplies, largely because of corruption. This not only calls into question how much extra food China truly has but also undermines public confidence in the government. In the case of a conflict, citizens conscious of the long-term disruption to come would begin panic buying and hoarding supplies. Whatever reserves do exist would eventually run out, with members of the elite likely being prioritized.

If the conflict dragged on, the situation would become increasingly dire. Agricultural producers, especially small farms, would be forced to cull their herds. Poultry, which requires especially high soybean content in its feed, would be the first casualty, which would then threaten the egg supply. Pork would be next. Cooking oils would grow scarce and expensive, making it difficult for restaurants and households to function. Eventually, public patience would wear thin: hoarding, protests over prices and shortages, and local officials diverting supplies could put mounting pressure on Beijing to end the war.

Unlike China’s vulnerabilities in energy or technology, the fragilities of the food system are endemic. If a conflict were to break out in the Pacific, the United States would not need to institute a blockade against China’s agricultural supplies or gather an international coalition to sanction China’s soybean imports. Soaring insurance costs and ship owners’ reluctance to sail into a war zone would be enough to squeeze the flow, and the Chinese government would have little recourse to stop it.

If Chinese leaders believed that they could achieve victory quickly and with few shots fired, they might take the bet. Deterrence, then, will require convincing them that they cannot. The United States, Taiwan, and other governments should invest in drones, rebuild munitions stockpiles, and ramp up messaging to China that a long, drawn-out conflict is likely. As long as China’s leaders believe that a war would be indecisive in its early stages, the threat of domestic crisis would be a powerful reason not to start one.

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

Demographic Decline, Political Paralysis, and the Fate of Great Powers

What Happens When Oceans Are Up for Grabs

#6017. Can Washington Afford to Deter Chinese Cyber Activity?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Can Washington Afford to Deter Chinese Cyber Activity?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

In August, the U.S. Justice Department and the FBI dismantled the online infrastructure behind two hacking platforms operated by a Chinese state-sponsored group. It had been targeting sensitive U.S. government networks since at least 2018. In October, the agencies seized hacking tools used by another such group whose operations the U.S. government had previously disrupted in 2024.

Washington can deny Beijing’s hackers access to harmful capabilities. But the longevity and resurgence of the hackers’ campaigns raise a hard truth: Disruption alone cannot reverse the strategic advantages generated by years of Chinese cyber activity.

In August, the U.S. Justice Department and the FBI dismantled the online infrastructure behind two hacking platforms operated by a Chinese state-sponsored group. It had been targeting sensitive U.S. government networks since at least 2018. In October, the agencies seized hacking tools used by another such group whose operations the U.S. government had previously disrupted in 2024.

Washington can deny Beijing’s hackers access to harmful capabilities. But the longevity and resurgence of the hackers’ campaigns raise a hard truth: Disruption alone cannot reverse the strategic advantages generated by years of Chinese cyber activity.

The conventional U.S. approach to cyber deterrence has treated Chinese operations as individual security incidents. But to deter China from accruing advantages through cyber activity, the United States must change the incentives of a vast hacking ecosystem built around the Chinese state in order to counteract its impacts on the United States’ military, technology, and trade.

A shift is now underway in Washington. In March, the U.S. government’s 2026 Cyber Strategy promised sustained and aggressive action through public-private partnerships, which would combine offensive and defensive capabilities with economic and legal countermeasures. Recent reports from the Council on Foreign Relations (CFR) and Britain’s Royal United Services Institute similarly advocate for coordinating cyber operations with other instruments of state power.

Such proposals appear to recognize that disrupting individual operations has not changed China’s broader cyber behavior. But while combining instruments may help disrupt individual operations and parts of the ecosystem, they may still fail to credibly deter the broader pattern of activity that generates cumulative advantage.

Therein lies the more troubling possibility: that the United States cannot credibly deter the broad pattern of Chinese cyber activity at a price that it is prepared—or willing—to sustain.

China’s recent cyber campaigns have advantages beyond individual intrusions. In 2023, for example, the Volt Typhoon campaign secured access to U.S. infrastructure in Guam that would be critical for U.S. operations in a future Taiwan contingency. While the United States removed some of the original footholds, the Justice Department has warned that the target infrastructure is still vulnerable; in early 2025, security analysts found that a Volt Typhoon-linked group had steadily penetrated U.S. utilities through that year. Disrupting individual intrusions had not prevented China from prepositioning for future disruption.

The year after the Guam intrusion, a campaign attributed to the Salt Typhoon hacker network compromised U.S. telecommunications networks, allowing Chinese actors to collect sensitive information from surveillance systems. More than a year after the intrusion was first disclosed, the campaign was discovered to be surveilling congressional staffers’ communications. It is still unclear whether Salt Typhoon infrastructure has been removed from the affected networks or whether it continues to accumulate intelligence.

A broad campaign of deterrence has proved to be largely ineffective, but it was once possible to think in narrower terms. In 2015, then-U.S. President Barack Obama and Chinese President Xi Jinping agreed that neither side would conduct or knowingly support cyber-enabled theft of intellectual property for commercial advantage. Security firms subsequently reported a decline in detected Chinese intrusions against U.S. companies (although the decline had begun before the agreement, and its causes remain contested).

The agreement did not end Chinese cyber espionage. It did, however, identify a relatively narrow category of conduct—commercial theft—that the two governments could explicitly agree to restrain, as opposed to the broader range of purposes that Chinese cyber operations now serve, from espionage to conflict prepositioning.

Washington no longer faces a discrete category of conduct around which to organize a deterrence effort. Today, it must determine which activities deserve a response, how much future risk they pose, and what consequences might discourage further operations. A response calibrated to an intrusion’s immediate effects may miss its future significance. Punishing every instance of access that might someday be used disruptively, meanwhile, would be difficult to sustain.

Similarly, punishing any single actor or intermediary that enables Chinese cyber operations is no longer enough to deter the wider ecosystem. Contractors disappear and reemerge frequently. The company disrupted in October provided hacking services to the People’s Liberation Army and the Ministry of State Security (MSS); others have conducted intrusions for both the MSS and the Ministry of Public Security. Rather than relying on single, self-contained operations, Chinese security organizations draw on overlapping external providers, creating an ecosystem that can regenerate operational capacity even amid disruptions.

Washington thinks offensive cyber power will restore deterrence. It’s mistaken.

Allies fear that Washington is retreating from leadership at the worst possible time.

For Washington, sanctions, export restrictions, criminal investigations, and public exposure could threaten the commercial interests and personal security of these actors. Rewards and protections encouraging people inside the enabling network to disclose information or defect, as proposed by the CFR report, could change incentives within the ecosystem, not merely punish its visible members. Pressure could make providers more cautious, expose links between companies and state agencies, and make operations harder to mount. At the state level, a credible threat of coordinated economic and diplomatic consequences might also discourage activities that Beijing considers too costly.

Yet such proposals contain significant shortcomings. Many enablers operate inside China, where they have little exposure to U.S. markets or financial systems, and their main sources of income come from state customers. In the past, sanctions have dismantled individual companies fronting the state. They would continue to have little lasting effect on companies that are dependent on Chinese security agencies, since Beijing can fund, protect, or replace providers when their work serves a strategic purpose.

Crucially, imposing costs on a contractor changes Beijing’s calculation only if the contractor’s financial, operational, or reputational losses degrade a longer-term strategic capability, threatens to expose other state-sponsored operations, or makes replacement offensive capabilities more costly for the wider ecosystem. If a state agency can absorb the loss and find another supplier, then Washington will have spent resources on punishing a node without changing the demand for its services. Previous attempts to identify and disrupt such valuable nodes have simply resulted in a temporary lull before resurgence.

To put meaningful pressure on Beijing, Washington would need to credibly signal that specific Chinese cyber activities would trigger costs substantial enough to change Chinese leaders’ calculations. That would require coordinated measures in more visible areas of strategic competition, such as restrictions on semiconductor exports or access to U.S. markets.

But China could retaliate in areas of competition where it retains leverage, such as by further restricting U.S. access to rare earth and critical mineral supplies, disrupting U.S. defense production. In that case, efforts to protect national security by deterring Chinese cyber activity could ultimately undermine it elsewhere.

Washington would thus need to accept immediate economic costs in return for uncertain deterrent effects for as long as necessary, when even sustained pressure may not stop the wider accumulation of access and intelligence. For the United States, China’s cyber operations may simply not hold enough strategic or political significance to warrant that economic price.

That leaves Washington with a dilemma. When espionage today can become disruption tomorrow, the value of deterring a wider range of cyber activity must be weighed against the costs of sustaining those restrictions and absorbing countermeasures. The challenge is to cast a wide enough net to address the sources of future risk while keeping threats specific and credible enough to influence behavior.

Accepting these risks does not have to mean abandoning deterrence; Washington can still seek to prevent China from using its access to U.S. infrastructure to disrupt essential services or pressure U.S. leaders during a crisis. But it should not confuse deterring a specific act with deterring the wider pattern of activity that accumulates strategic advantages over time.

Access may be the most valuable when it remains concealed, preserving China’s choice of whether and when to leverage it and leaving Washington to bear the costs of preparing for multiple possible outcomes. The more that Beijing believes Washington will tolerate continued accumulation, the greater its incentive to preserve that position and scale it—as the price for the United States only grows higher with time.

Ahana Datta Fasel is the author of Full Stack Spies: Cyber Espionage in the Age of US-China Competition (Hurst). She advises U.K. Research and Innovation on national security and defense.

#6014. Why Kushner and Witkoff Keep Failing

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Why Kushner and Witkoff Keep Failing

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff are unofficial presidential envoys, personally selected by U.S. President Donald Trump to handle critical negotiations over Ukraine, Gaza, and Iran. While it’s safe to say neither was selected after a long and extensive search (Kushner is Trump’s son-in-law and Witkoff is a longtime friend and golfing buddy), let’s be charitable and assume that they have been genuinely trying to reach agreements that would advance U.S. interests. The question is: Why have they accomplished so little?

Look at the record: The supposed peace deal over Gaza is a sham, as Israel continues to bomb Gaza with impunity, expand its territorial control there, and limit relief aid while tolerating (if not actively helping) Israeli settlers’ violent campaign against Palestinians in the West Bank. Genuine peace may not have been their real objective, however, as Kushner and Witkoff are both ardent Israel supporters who were even less likely to put serious pressure on Israel than former U.S. President Joe Biden (which is saying something). In this view, the Gaza agreement was a Potemkin peace deal whose main goal was to free Israeli hostages held by Hamas and get the conflict off the front pages while tying Israel’s hands as little as possible. If so, then one might concede that they accomplished their objective. But if the goal was peace, they failed.

Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff are unofficial presidential envoys, personally selected by U.S. President Donald Trump to handle critical negotiations over Ukraine, Gaza, and Iran. While it’s safe to say neither was selected after a long and extensive search (Kushner is Trump’s son-in-law and Witkoff is a longtime friend and golfing buddy), let’s be charitable and assume that they have been genuinely trying to reach agreements that would advance U.S. interests. The question is: Why have they accomplished so little?

Look at the record: The supposed peace deal over Gaza is a sham, as Israel continues to bomb Gaza with impunity, expand its territorial control there, and limit relief aid while tolerating (if not actively helping) Israeli settlers’ violent campaign against Palestinians in the West Bank. Genuine peace may not have been their real objective, however, as Kushner and Witkoff are both ardent Israel supporters who were even less likely to put serious pressure on Israel than former U.S. President Joe Biden (which is saying something). In this view, the Gaza agreement was a Potemkin peace deal whose main goal was to free Israeli hostages held by Hamas and get the conflict off the front pages while tying Israel’s hands as little as possible. If so, then one might concede that they accomplished their objective. But if the goal was peace, they failed.

Similarly, their efforts to negotiate an agreement with Iran before the war never went anywhere, in part because these two amateur diplomats apparently didn’t understand some critical technical details regarding Iran’s nuclear program but also because Trump and Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu decided to launch a war while talks were underway. As with Gaza, the negotiations may have been something of a sham from the start, intended not to reach a lasting agreement but to allow time to prepare for another round of war.

Russia’s war in Ukraine is still raging, too—a direct refutation of Trump’s campaign boast that he could end it in 24 hours. Witkoff and Kushner have met with Russian officials on multiple occasions and offered increasingly generous inducements, even as the administration keeps pressuring Kyiv to cut a deal. The result has been not peace but more war, including expanding attacks on civilian targets in Ukraine and Russia alike.

If judged by whether their efforts have led to peace, in short, Kushner and Witkoff are 0 for 3. To be fair, handling all three of these conflicts at once would tax the abilities of an Otto von Bismarck or a Henry Kissinger, and trying to conduct complex and difficult diplomacy on behalf of an erratic president who is more interested in appearances than achievements would be a challenge for any negotiator. But these handicaps don’t fully explain their repeated failures.

One can think of several reasons why their efforts have been unsuccessful, but a big part of the problem is their overarching worldview. Kushner and Witkoff and Trump are all real estate developers who are preoccupied with making money. That’s the only thing that really matters to them, which is why Trump seems more interested in monetizing the presidency than he is in actually governing and why Kushner may have pocketed billions of dollars in secret payments from the United Arab Emirates and Saudi Arabia (an accusation he denies). If you assume that everyone else is also on the make, then the way to get to yes is to offer your counterparts a sweet deal that will make them richer. In this world, everyone has their price, and the trick is to figure out what that price is and then convince them you can meet it.

This one-dimensional perspective was already evident in Trump’s first term. It lay behind Kushner’s belief that the key to Middle East peace was to ignore the Palestinian desire for statehood or political rights and focus instead on promoting business deals among Israel, some wealthy Gulf states, and (eventually) Saudi Arabia. That same instinct underlay Trump’s belief that he could get North Korean leader Kim Jong Un to give up his arsenal of nuclear weapons by offering an alluring vision of ritzy resorts and luxury condos along North Korean beaches, an idea later recycled as a potential blueprint for Gaza. And it is obviously central to recent attempts to persuade the Kremlin to end its war in Ukraine by buying some Russian diesel fuel, letting Russian companies help develop gas fields in Alaska, talking up lucrative future business deals, and ending Moscow’s economic isolation from the West.

The obvious flaw in this worldview is its failure to recognize that the top priority for smart political leaders isn’t money or wealth—it’s national security. Security is more important because without it, one’s wealth is vulnerable: Having all the money in the world is useless if someone stronger can take it from you. Look at what happened to Kuwait in 1990, when Iraqi leader Saddam Hussein decided to invade the wealthy emirate, simultaneously eliminating one of his main creditors and gaining control of resources that would have increased Iraq’s GDP by roughly 40 percent. And his gambit would have worked had a coalition of outside powers not ridden to Kuwait’s rescue.

Wealth is far from irrelevant, of course, and greed can be a powerful motivator, too. I don’t doubt that Russian President Vladimir Putin would be happy to pocket all those blandishments that Kushner and Witkoff keep dangling as part of an eventual peace deal in Ukraine. But if all he cared about were money, he wouldn’t have invaded Ukraine in the first place, and he wouldn’t have continued to wage war there at considerable economic and human cost. Unless and until the security concerns (whether exaggerated or not) that drove his decision to invade are met, offering him a bunch of economic goodies isn’t going to end the war.

The same is true for Kyiv, of course: If money were all that mattered, the smart move would be to accept the loss of Crimea and the Donbas and hold out for a big aid package to rebuild Ukraine’s shattered economy. But Ukrainians don’t want to surrender lands they regard as rightly theirs, even for a price. Equally important, they don’t want to live in fear of future Russian threats. Thus, a future peace arrangement would have to meet both sides’ security concerns as well as their economic desires.

Nations also care about preserving their identity and cultural autonomy, and those living under a foreign yoke often want to govern themselves. As a result, these groups will sometimes make enormous sacrifices to try to establish their own state. Palestinians won’t abandon their national identity or desire for political rights in exchange for a lucrative economic package (even assuming one were genuinely on offer), and neither will any number of other aspiring national movements. That same sense of national pride also helps explain why bombing other countries to topple a regime or get it to surrender rarely works, even when it is coupled with the prospect of future economic benefits. On the contrary, such actions tend to rally the population behind leaders who might otherwise lack broad support and make them even less willing to compromise.

International politics, in short, is not like real estate development because relations among states take place in an arena where there is no central authority and where some states have the ability and the will to use violence to get what they want. In such a world, states have powerful incentives to guarantee their own security first and then worry about how to become more prosperous. A solid grasp of how business works and experience negotiating complicated deals can be an asset in diplomacy (as former Secretaries of State George Shultz and James Baker demonstrated)—but only if you understand the critical differences between these two realms. Regrettably, Kushner and Witkoff (and Trump) show no sign of having understood the distinction or of having learned very much from their experiences to date. But they (or their families) are making some serious bank while these conflicts rage on, which may have been the real goal all along.

Stephen M. Walt is a columnist at Foreign Policy and the Robert and Renée Belfer professor of international relations at Harvard University. Walt is the author of The New Geopolitics, a five-part newsletter masterclass breaking down the biggest concepts you should use to navigate today’s world. Bluesky: @stephenwalt.bsky.social X: @stephenwalt

#6012. How America Can Counter Chinese Coercion

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

THEODORE BUNZEL is Managing Director and head of Lazard Geopolitical Advisory. He has worked in the political section of the U.S. Embassy in Moscow and at the U.S. Treasury Department.

EDWARD FISHMAN is Senior Fellow and Director of the Maurice R. Greenberg Center for Geoeconomics at the Council on Foreign Relations. He is the author of Chokepoints: American Power in the Age of Economic Warfare.

ADDAR LEVI is Deputy General Counsel and Global Head of Regulatory Affairs at Lazard. She was previously the Acting General Counsel of the U.S. Department of the Treasury.

The Case for an Economic Security Alliance

Theodore Bunzel, Edward Fishman, and Addar Levi

Over the past year and a half, China has shown the world the power of its economic arsenal—starting with the United States. In April 2025, Beijing retaliated forcefully against U.S. tariffs, imposing matching across-the-board levies and export controls on rare-earth elements. This counterpunch rattled bond markets and disrupted auto supply chains, forcing Washington to seek a truce that persists to this day.

Next, China targeted Japan. After Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi described a potential Chinese naval blockade of Taiwan as a “survival-threatening situation” that could justify deploying Japan’s Self-Defense Forces, in November 2025, Beijing imposed suffocating bans on rare-earth exports to the country. Reluctant to reinflame tensions with Beijing, Washington offered nothing more than token support. Tokyo has held firm, refusing to walk back Takaichi’s statement; the restrictions remain in force, and Japan’s mineral stockpiles are dwindling.

Then Europe came under fire. Fed up with a trade deficit with China that had reached $1.1 billion per day, the European Union began exploring new tariffs to counter Chinese overcapacity in May. As China threatened “resolute countermeasures,” French President Emmanuel Macron urged U.S. President Donald Trump at the G-7 summit in June to join forces in addressing global imbalances. But the United States elected to sit on the sidelines again, and Europe blinked, opting for talks with Beijing, with an October deadline set by Europe to take concrete steps toward reducing China’s trade surplus. The EU has since been in tense negotiations with Beijing over reducing exports of hybrid vehicles, chemicals, and machinery, taking on the threat of Chinese retaliation alone.

China, in other words, is exploiting the disunity between the United States and its partners. In a divide-and-conquer strategy that mirrors the Trump administration’s own globe-spanning tariff campaign, Beijing has broken the Western alliance into more manageable pieces, allowing it to fight actors one on one. Its market heft and control over critical minerals have been decisive, and as its fear of retaliation has diminished, it has rolled out more aggressive export restrictions and sanctions. In 2023 and 2024, Beijing added only five Western companies to its Unreliable Entity List, exposing them to financial and legal penalties; in 2025 alone, it added 76.

There is still hope for the United States and its allies—if they can relearn to act in concert. These countries should build a formal economic security alliance that establishes collective defense against economic coercion. The foundation of this alliance would be a pledge modeled on NATO’s Article 5, which considers an attack on one as an attack on all, in this case promising a joint response to economic coercion against any member of the alliance. Over time, this alliance could grow to address other economic challenges shared by members, just as NATO began as a security guarantee before evolving into a more integrated and expansive military alliance. It could adopt joint tariffs against Chinese overcapacity and currency manipulation, coordinate investment restrictions and export controls on advanced technology, and cooperate on critical minerals and AI development.

Amid transatlantic squabbles and European calls for “strategic autonomy,” a Western economic security alliance may seem more out of reach today than ever before. But enduring interests can outweigh temporary political friction. With the right policy shifts and trust-building measures—starting with the removal of Trump’s tariffs on allies and legislation that limits the U.S. president’s ability to reimpose them in the future—Washington can forge such a partnership and use it to check Chinese coercion.

The United States, Europe, and Japan are all confronting a major shared challenge: Beijing is intent on making the rest of the world more dependent on China and China less dependent on the rest of the world. Beijing subsidizes domestic production far beyond local demand, undervalues its currency by 20 to 30 percent, and enacts coercive economic measures—such as export controls and sanctions—to gain leverage over Western governments and companies and undermine policies it deems against its interest. The country’s resulting industrial overcapacity and surging exports, epitomized by a record $1.2 trillion trade surplus last year, threatens U.S., European, and Japanese industrial competitiveness and jobs. Beijing’s record of weaponizing its strengths also means that Chinese advancements in frontier technologies, such as artificial intelligence and connected vehicles, which continuously share data with external devices, pose security threats to Washington and all its partners.

Individually, the United States and its allies may struggle to match Beijing’s economic leverage, particularly its chokehold on rare-earth elements. But collectively, they are far stronger. The United States, Europe, and Japan alone produce around three times the amount of economic output that China does. They are home to the vast majority of the world’s most sophisticated companies. They also control critical inputs that China depends on, including lithography machines, specialized machinery for industrial production, chemicals, and aircraft engines. Despite efforts to reduce its dependence on the dollar, Beijing remains exposed to U.S. financial sanctions; only about 30 percent of China’s trade is settled in renminbi, and most of the rest is settled in dollars. The scholars Stephen Brooks and Ben Vagle have estimated that if the United States and its allies were to fully decouple from China, the harm to China’s GDP in the short run would be five to 11 times larger than that to U.S. GDP.

China is also more vulnerable than it appears. Despite an official GDP growth rate of five percent last year, the economic research firm Rhodium Group estimated that the country’s actual growth rate was 2.5 to 3.0 percent. China is undergoing a prolonged real estate crisis, state-driven overinvestment, and anemic domestic demand. With Beijing unwilling to address weak consumption, exports now fuel more than half of the country’s growth, according to Rhodium’s numbers, making access to Western markets China’s economic Achilles’ heel.

If history is any guide, these Western states should be able to coordinate to use their collective power. During the Cold War, they came together to form the Coordinating Committee for Multilateral Export Controls to restrict sales of military and dual-use technology to the Soviet Union. After the 1973 Arab oil embargo, Canada, Japan, the United States, and a large group of European countries established the International Energy Agency, which required members to share reserves with any member affected by a supply cut—effectively a mutual defense pact for energy. And during the 2008 financial crisis, the U.S. Federal Reserve and other allied central banks coordinated rate cuts and established emergency swap lines to avert another Great Depression.

Yet in recent years, collective economic initiatives among Western allies have been underwhelming. In 2021, the U.S.-EU Trade and Technology Council was created to coordinate industrial policy and export controls toward China, but it largely devolved into a talk shop. In 2023, the EU established its Anti-Coercion Instrument after China boycotted Lithuanian goods in response to the opening of a Taiwanese Representative Office in Vilnius, but it has yet to use it. That same year, the G-7 announced a new Coordination Platform on Economic Coercion, which pledged members to warn each other and cooperate against shared economic threats, such as China’s nonmarket policies and economic coercion on critical minerals. But the platform contained no binding commitments and has largely been ignored.

These initiatives foundered from a lack of urgency and a reluctance among European countries to antagonize China, which is one of their largest trading partners. They were also constrained by disputes between European countries and the Biden administration over steel and aluminum tariffs and the large domestic subsidies in the U.S. Inflation Reduction Act. But Trump’s second-term policies have been the nail in the coffin. U.S. tariffs and threats to annex Greenland have rattled trust among American allies. Washington’s trade truce with Beijing has dispelled illusions that the transatlantic partners form a united front and made European partners doubt that the United States will come to their aid. Calls are indeed growing within Europe for de-risking and “digital sovereignty”—but from the United States, not China.

These calls will not be easy to quiet, even if Trump is followed by a president focused less on tariffs and more on building alliances. During the Biden administration, allies told U.S. officials that they would not commit to larger-scale economic coordination on shared challenges regarding China, Ukraine, or the Middle East because they were concerned that a future U.S. president could erase the progress. Europe’s concerns are well founded. As American politics has become increasingly polarized, more power has amassed in the executive branch. Congress, which historically provided ballast in U.S. foreign policy, has been absent. When Trump announced sweeping duties under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act against U.S. allies in April 2025, Congress did effectively nothing. When the Supreme Court ruled these tariffs as unconstitutional, the administration pivoted to other tariff authorities, and again, Congress stood by. Rebuilding trust will therefore require an institutional remedy that goes beyond communiqués and professions that “America is back.”

There is reason to think that the United States can repair its ties. The country still has a strong economic relationship with Europe. The value of cross-border mergers and acquisitions and investments across the Atlantic last year remained mostly normal, and total trade in goods and services between the United States and the EU increased by nearly six percent over the course of 2025. There is also little evidence a major economic rupture is coming. Despite frustrations with Washington in European capitals, European businesses want to maintain ties with the United States.

More important, Europe has finally awoken to the economic threat from China, because the surge of auto and industrial exports from China to Europe poses an existential challenge for the continent’s economic model. Chinese auto exports to Europe increased by 29 percent in 2025 and by over 60 percent year-over-year in 2026 as European auto exports to China have flatlined. Germany’s mighty auto industry alone lost 50,000 jobs in 2025. Even Volkswagen, which lobbied against the EU’s tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles in 2024, has changed its tune. In July, its CEO all but urged the EU to extend tariffs to Chinese plug-in hybrids. Germany, which shed 74,000 industrial workers in non-auto sectors in 2025, has begun quietly identifying chokepoints it can use as leverage over China. Despite a provisional agreement with Beijing to curb Chinese hybrid vehicle exports in October, Brussels appears poised to unveil new measures against China on chemicals and machinery in the coming months as it finally grapples with the threat of Chinese industrial overcapacity.

These challenges will only get worse in the coming years and make Europe more likely to seek the United States’ help. And in Washington, the notion of a united Western front against Beijing has bipartisan resonance. As Kurt Campbell and Rush Doshi have recently argued in Foreign Affairs, even if the Trump administration continues to alienate some European partners, shared interests will keep them open to allied cooperation when the next administration comes.

When it does, the most important step will be terminating every tariff—issued pursuant to Section 232, Section 301, Section 338, or Section 201—and pending investigation against any ally that was imposed during the second Trump administration. This would present both a unilateral demonstration of good faith and an opening gambit for negotiations on an economic security alliance. It would be a decisive break from Trump’s tariff policy and a rejection of Biden’s halfway model, which mostly turned tariffs into quotas and suspensions. It would be both politically popular, considering Americans’ dismal view of tariffs, and good for the economy, as the removal of duties could lower prices and accelerate growth. And because Trump’s tariffs have all been enacted via executive action, they can also be terminated that way.

As part of this first phase, the White House should also pledge support to any ally facing Chinese coercion, by providing targeted economic assistance or access to relevant stockpiles, such as the rare earths the Trump administration has begun amassing in its U.S. Strategic Critical Minerals Reserve. If the president made good on such pledges, it would demonstrate concrete benefits to potential members and build confidence in the alliance’s viability.

These unilateral measures would set the table for a formal economic security treaty. That treaty’s foundation would include an economic mutual defense commitment, mimicking NATO’s Article 5, which would go a long way toward restoring Western escalation dominance over China and therefore defanging Beijing’s threats. Such a pledge would have both defensive and offensive components. For defense, members would commit to providing targeted allies with economic assistance, preferential market access, and access to stockpiles of critical goods. Offensively, members would respond to coercion against an ally by applying joint sanctions, tariffs, or export controls. The United States, the EU, and Japan should form the core of this alliance given their individual economic and strategic heft, but other like-minded partners, such as Australia, Canada, and the United Kingdom, should be welcome to join as well.

Ensuring the alliance’s longevity will require more than goodwill gestures carried out by executive action. The most durable approach would be a congressional-executive agreement backed by legislation, which would embed the alliance firmly in U.S. law and make it harder to unwind. Critically, this legislation should protect allies from the whims of future presidents. Over the years, Congress has delegated broad powers to the executive to impose tariffs for national security and other reasons—powers that recent administrations have used with reckless abandon. Congressional treaty legislation could codify the removal of these excessive tariffs and restrict any additional duties on alliance members unless approved by Congress. This self-limiting measure would give allies confidence that the bargain would survive changes of U.S. administrations. To address the possibility that novel legal theories could somehow be used by the executive branch in the future, the treaty legislation should also enact an automatic sunset after 60 days on any new duties on alliance members unless Congress affirmatively acts to retain them. Finally, the bill would pre-authorize rapid assistance to any ally facing foreign economic coercion, giving the executive the authority to honor the pact’s collective defense commitment.

These measures would be important not only to improve trust between governments but also between governments and the private sector. Firms make investment decisions based partly on whether they expect relations between countries to remain stable and predictable—often on a five- or ten-year time horizon, beyond any one U.S. presidential term. Today, in light of the transatlantic friction over trade and technology, many companies are debating whether to create separate operational and supply chain ecosystems for Europe and the United States. By constraining a future U.S. president’s ability to impose new tariffs on alliance members, an economic security pact would give companies confidence to build lasting economic links across the alliance system, even in the absence of a formal free-trade agreement.

Enacting ambitious legislation is always difficult, but there is reason to believe that U.S. lawmakers would be supportive of such an alliance. Chinese economic coercion is a bipartisan concern. Although some members of Congress may be wary of coming out against tariffs, the tool has become significantly less popular during Trump’s second term, and reclaiming congressional authority over tariff policy would serve Congress’s institutional interests. In a February survey by the Harris Poll, 72 percent of Americans agreed that Trump’s tariffs have had more of a negative than a positive impact, with 60 percent of Republicans agreeing that they have negatively impacted consumers. Tariffs on allies are especially unpopular: in an Ipsos poll conducted in August, Americans opposed placing additional tariffs on Canada by nearly three to one. Tariffs have also become closely associated with inflation. In an August poll conducted by the Council on Foreign Relations and Morning Consult, 75 percent of Americans reported being concerned about the price of food and groceries, and 83 percent of that group said that tariffs had increased those prices. With affordability now Americans’ number one concern, lawmakers have strong incentives to vote for legislation limiting tariffs.

The United States would have to lead the way for an economic security alliance, but just as important would be negotiations among potential members over the rules governing the pact itself. The agreement should spell out how collective defense would work in practice, specifying how allies can facilitate sharing commercial information and economic intelligence; who determines when economic coercion has occurred; and what kind of assistance, offensive and defensive, members can expect in such an event. If the alliance were successful in expanding its membership and scope, it could establish an independent secretariat to help it function, but the goal should be to make commitments credible without creating another sprawling international bureaucracy.

Collective economic defense would be just the starting point. Over time, the alliance could adapt to address a broader set of economic security challenges. On Chinese overcapacity, members could coordinate tariffs on the sectors most distorted by nonmarket practices such as subsidies and preferential financing, preventing Beijing from simply redirecting surplus exports from one allied market to another, or leverage a common baseline tariff on Chinese goods to compel Beijing to strengthen its undervalued currency. The same logic applies to export controls, which Western countries vary widely in applying. To break reliance on Chinese critical minerals, members could use the alliance to simplify the confusing set of international critical mineral initiatives underway by streamlining financing, making long-term purchase commitments, investing in processing, and building up stockpiles.

In this way, collective economic defense could follow a path similar to NATO. The North Atlantic alliance began with a relatively simple security guarantee that evolved into a cohesive military bloc: common institutions, shared planning, interoperable capabilities, and habits of cooperation that would have been difficult to create at the outset. An economic security alliance could evolve in much the same way. A credible promise of mutual defense would create the confidence needed for governments and firms to integrate more deeply over time.

To be sure, an economic zone for the free world would be an ambitious undertaking, especially at a moment of unusually low trust between the United States and its allies. But the United States, Europe, and Japan do not need to agree on all matters of economic statecraft to build such a space, nor do they need to trust that future governments will always share the same priorities. What they need are institutions that allow them to cooperate despite those disagreements and that make their most important commitments durable enough to survive political change.

Moments of geopolitical rupture have provided fertile ground for institution building in the past. NATO emerged amid doubts about whether U.S. support would continue after World War II. In 1956, the Suez crisis badly strained U.S.-British relations, but in the years following, Washington and London rebuilt their partnership and established the 1958 Mutual Defense Agreement, which solidified nuclear cooperation and further bound the two countries together. The economic turmoil of the early 1970s, including the United States’ controversial exit from the Bretton Woods system and the Arab oil embargo, ended up pushing the major industrial democracies toward regular economic coordination that evolved into the G-7. In each case, crisis created opportunity.

Today, the hardest part for Washington will be accepting constraints on its freedom to act unilaterally. Presidents of both parties have long been wary of legislative checks on executive power out of fear that binding their hands would signal weakness. But if American power is constrained by predictable rules, allies will be more comfortable depending on it. If allies fear American economic power, they will hedge against it. Washington, in reserving the right to turn its economic weapons against its allies, will struggle to mobilize the collective weight of its alliances in markets, technology, and capital.

China’s divide-and-conquer strategy has capitalized on distrust within the U.S. alliance system. Restoring that trust will require Washington to make commitments that persist no matter who occupies the White House. The central wager of an economic security alliance is that the United States can gain leverage over China by surrendering some leverage over its allies. An economic zone for the free world can turn the tide against China, but only if the United States ties itself to the mast.
