#6000. What Sanctions Get Right

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

S. C. M. PAINE is William S. Sims University Professor of History and Grand Strategy Emerita at the U.S. Naval War College.

ANAND TOPRANI is a Jeane Kirkpatrick Fellow at the American Enterprise Institute.

They are the co-editors of The Strategy of Sanctions: From Antiquity to the Twenty-First Century.

The United States frequently uses sanctions or embargoes to rein in malign actors. When policymakers become dissatisfied with the results, they often embrace military action next. Washington, for example, applied sanctions on Iraq for more than a decade before invading the country in 2003. It did the same with Iran, using sanctions to pressure the government into nuclear negotiations in the 2010s and then, after withdrawing from the resulting agreement in 2018, going to war with Tehran in February 2026.

Washington follows this pattern of behavior because sanctions alone rarely—if ever—make a problem disappear, such as by toppling the government responsible. This frustrates Americans, who tend to seek simple, quick, and permanent fixes to foreign policy problems. Many interpret the persistence of these problems as evidence that sanctions are futile. But the effectiveness of sanctions is measured in crises that do not occur—the nuclear test that never happens, for instance, or the shipping lane that remains undisturbed. These successes are invisible, but an adversary’s continuing provocations are not. As a result, American officials often conclude that it is necessary to turn to war.

This logic reflects a misunderstanding about how sanctions work. Sanctions may not be able to achieve maximalist aims, yet when they are deployed in concert with other policy tools, they can help manage problems. They do this by reliably depressing the target country’s economic growth over time. Although this hurts innocent civilians in the targeted countries as their leaders shift the costs from regime supporters to the politically disenfranchised, no government can escape the inexorable math of slowing economic growth. If an economy starts out with a three percent growth rate—roughly the global average—it will double in size in around 23 years. But if sanctions reduce annual growth to one percent, doubling in size will take 70 years, three times as long. Just compare the North Korean and South Korean economies, or look at nighttime satellite imagery of the Korean Peninsula, to see the compounding effects of sanctions over generations. Although sanctions may not change the targeted regime’s aspirations, they reduce its financial wherewithal and may deter other governments from emulating the behavior that precipitated the sanctions.

Wars can overthrow adversarial regimes, but usually only after years of fighting, and fixing the damage afterward takes considerable time and money. Too often the United States misidentifies its foreign policy problems as solvable on the cheap. It took an Allied occupation force of over 1.5 million soldiers to garrison Germany, a country of 80 million, after World War II. Fast forward 60 years, and Washington was not interested in raising the numbers of troops necessary to subdue Iraq, a country of nearly 30 million. Americans today are likewise unwilling to make sacrifices on the scale necessary to vanquish Iran, with its 90 million people—much less China, with its 1.4 billion. The challenges these countries might pose are too large to solve at an acceptable cost. In such cases, sanctions offer an economically sustainable strategy to manage troublesome situations in the long term.

According to the conventional wisdom about sanctions, these measures discourage unsavory behavior by raising its economic costs. If force becomes necessary, they also weaken the adversary in advance of war. This logic has endured since 1935, when the League of Nations sanctioned Italy for invading Ethiopia, in the hope that doing so would induce Italy to call off its invasion or at least temper its ambitions. Yet because the countries imposing sanctions were unwilling to include vital commodities such as oil and coal on the list of sanctioned items, the effort was ineffective.

Sanctions have evolved since 1935. Today, unlike broad sanctions that inflict significant collateral damage, so-called smart sanctions target specific institutions or individuals and are enforced through the banking system, blocking financial transactions linked to sanctioned activities. UN sanctions against Iraq starting in 1990 were, in a sense, the last gasp of the old sanctions regime. Their lifting was contingent on Iraq’s dismantling its weapons of mass destruction program and agreeing to respect Kuwaiti sovereignty. Because verification of the former proved difficult, many of the sanctions remained in place after a 1991 armistice, and they continued through the 2003 Iraq war. After U.S.-led forces invaded Iraq in March 2003 and overthrew Saddam Hussein, it became apparent that the sanctions had been effective. Isolation from the global economy had prevented Iraq from restoring its economy, rebuilding its military, and resuming its development of weapons of mass destruction.

Between the two wars, however, many observers believed that international sanctions were unable to deter Iraqi aggression and neutralize its weapons programs. Worried that Iraq’s Sunni government still posed a threat to Kurds in the north and Shiites in the south, the United States, the United Kingdom, and, for a short time, France imposed two no-fly zones over those regions, without UN consent. In 1998, after Baghdad expelled UN weapons inspectors, the United States and the United Kingdom bombed Iraq. The same year, a group of hawkish scholars and former government officials sent an open letter to U.S. President Bill Clinton describing the sanctions-based Iraq policy as “dangerously inadequate.” Many of these signatories would return to government in the George W. Bush administration to implement their recommendations.

To a certain extent, the critics had a point. Political divisions within the United States and between Washington and its partners had constrained international efforts to contain Saddam. Arab governments may have been wary of hurting their relationships with the United States, but Arab publics were sick of Western imperialism. The growing U.S. military presence in the Middle East further antagonized both the anti-imperialist left and radical Islamists, and many Europeans resented American unilateralism.

Saddam also pushed doctored stories about civilian hardship arising from sanctions, which Western journalists readily covered. Iraqi suffering was very real, but it was largely a result of Saddam’s own policies, which channeled resources to his regime rather than to the civilian population. Starting in 1995, the UN’s oil-for-food program allowed Iraq to sell some oil to pay for civilian necessities, but Saddam used the profits to pay kickbacks instead. He signed lucrative oil deals with China, France, and Russia, enticing these permanent UN Security Council members to weaken their support for sanctions. It was around this time that the UN and civil society groups started calling for smart sanctions, to narrow the scope of economic punishment and avoid causing wide-scale economic pain, although it would take several more years for the United States to refine this tool.

In the meantime, in Iraq, sanctions and no-fly-zone enforcement became increasingly frustrating, costly, and unpopular. Because their main purpose was to prevent Iraq from developing weapons of mass destruction and funding malign activities such as international terrorism, they required constant vigilance. Then came the 9/11 attacks and the war in Afghanistan, where a U.S.-led coalition toppled the Taliban regime within months, suggesting that wars—at least those conducted by the highly trained, technologically advanced U.S. military—could solve problems, unlike the sanctions that left dictators in place.

When U.S. President George W. Bush announced the Iraq war on March 19, 2003, he described its goals as “to disarm Iraq, to free its people, and to defend the world from grave danger.” This danger was Iraq’s alleged stockpiles of weapons of mass destruction and its links to al-Qaeda, the group behind 9/11. At first, Washington intended to leave the task of reconstructing Iraq to the UN, but it had to abandon that strategy five months after the invasion when foreign jihadists bombed the UN’s local headquarters, killing the head of the mission and 22 others. Within weeks, the UN withdrew most of its 600 staff members, leaving the United States fully in charge.

Rather than ending the trouble Iraq caused, occupation created more problems. The war lent credence to al-Qaeda’s accusations that Washington oppressed Muslims, attracting would-be jihadists around the world to join the cause. In 2006, al-Qaeda’s bombing of the Golden Mosque in Samarra, a holy site for Shiites, triggered brutal Sunni–Shiite fighting. More than 4,000 Americans lost their lives over the course of the war, while more than 200,000 Iraqis lost theirs. After the U.S. withdrawal in 2011, much of the Middle East and North Africa went up in flames. Foreign jihadists and remnants of Saddam’s Baath Party formed the Islamic State, also known as ISIS, and overran much of Iraq and Syria in 2014 and 2015. U.S.- and Iranian-backed forces gradually and at great cost pushed the group out, but many of its fighters simply took up arms elsewhere. Some went to Sahelian Africa, exacerbating violence there. Others joined the opposition to Bashar al-Assad’s regime in Syria. And the refugee crisis sparked in part by the Islamic State’s initial expansion continues to roil Europe.

Whereas war brought spiraling instability to the Middle East, North Africa, and Europe, international sanctions were more effective than U.S. policymakers thought. While they were in place, Iraq was unable to build weapons of mass destruction, and it dismantled many of its existing programs to secure sanctions relief. The 2003 war was not nearly as successful and cost far more than sanctions enforcement. Washington went in with the aim of building a free and stable Iraq allied to the United States and Israel, but it quickly became clear that goal was out of reach.

Another unintended consequence of the war was the unleashing of Iran, which for years Saddam’s Iraq had kept in check. Iran and the United States had been at loggerheads since the Iranian revolution of 1978–79. In November 1979, Washington sanctioned Iran for taking the U.S. embassy’s staff members hostage, lifting them upon the staff’s release in 1981. The Reagan administration reimposed sanctions in 1984 in retaliation for Iran’s support for Hezbollah in Lebanon, only to violate them by selling arms to Iran (and using the funds to arm anticommunist Nicaraguan rebels, in defiance of Congress) in what became the Iran-contra scandal. The Clinton administration, concerned about Tehran’s nuclear ambitions, added sanctions between 1994 and 1996. After the International Atomic Energy Agency found Iran to be in noncompliance with the Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty in 2005, the Bush administration imposed additional sanctions, but they targeted specific entities, such as the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, rather than Iran as a whole to minimize the humanitarian costs. Then, in 2007, the world got behind a UN Security Council resolution sanctioning Iran to prevent it from acquiring nuclear weapons. Finally, in 2010 and 2011, the Obama administration, with congressional backing, limited Iran’s ability to sell oil and gas abroad, “virtually grinding the Iranian economy to halt,” as the president bragged.

The growing strength and scope of sanctions contributed to Iran’s election of a moderate president, Hassan Rouhani, in 2013 and empowered the Iranian factions advocating a negotiated settlement to the nuclear standoff. In 2015, Tehran agreed to a nuclear deal with the United States, China, France, Germany, Russia, the United Kingdom, and the EU. The agreement capped Iran’s uranium enrichment levels, reduced its operational centrifuge numbers, required Iran to repurpose its nuclear weapons development facilities, and established more rigorous international inspections to verify compliance. Sanctions relief would occur in tandem with verification over a 15-year period. Thereafter, either the signatories could negotiate a new agreement, or the sanctions would snap back into place. Critics complained, justifiably, that the 2015 deal did not prevent Iran from supporting proxy groups or limit its ballistic missile program. But the deal did offer a sustainable way to delay Iran’s nuclear weapons development and thereby manage the most important problem posed by a hostile Iranian regime. A more comprehensive solution, however desirable, was not feasible at the time.

The United States is discovering, yet again, that military action has delivered a far worse outcome than sanctions. After the first Trump administration withdrew from the nuclear deal in 2018, Iran restarted its weapons program. The second Trump administration and Israel went to war early this year in an ostensible effort to end Iran’s nuclear ambitions but proved unable to destroy all its nuclear sites. The United States and Israel assassinated Iran’s leadership, including the supreme leader and much of his family, yet the regime remains intact. Tehran has promised retribution for these killings, and there is no reason to think it will not follow through on the threat. Meanwhile, the war has wreaked global havoc with no end in sight. Iran responded to U.S. and Israeli attacks by effectively closing the Strait of Hormuz and striking oil, gas, and military infrastructure across the Middle East. The disruption of exports of petroleum, fertilizer, and helium has contributed to inflation, portends crop shortfalls, and even threatens computer chip shortages in countries around the world.

Although U.S. military casualties remain low, U.S. losses in destroyed infrastructure and depleted munitions stocks amount to billions of dollars, and some of it may be irreplaceable. The American military depends on satellite communications and radar to detect and target incoming enemy missiles and aircraft. Iran has heavily damaged or perhaps destroyed air defense systems the United States and its partners deployed at bases in the United Arab Emirates and Jordan, as well as two satellite communications terminals at a U.S. naval base in Bahrain. Iranian strikes also destroyed radomes, the outer shells that protect radar antennae, in Bahrain, Kuwait, and Saudi Arabia. The United States has lost at least 43 warplanes, including specialized refueling and surveillance aircraft and a surveillance plane worth $700 million. Many U.S. military bases are now inoperable, including the main regional naval base, in Bahrain. Because U.S. aircraft cannot operate safely from nearby bases, the war effort has relied on distant aircraft carriers positioned in the eastern Mediterranean and Arabian Seas, where they can avoid the dangers of Iranian missiles, drones, and mines.

Far from removing the Iranian regime, the war has demonstrated that it has the power to hold the global economy hostage. By comparison, the prewar sanctions that devastated Iran’s economy, hobbled its military, and discouraged it from seeking nuclear weapons were a bargain.

Sanctions have a far better track record than war in managing unsolvable problems at an acceptable price for a protracted period. With sanctions, the target country—including its civilian population, unfortunately—pays the costs. In war, by contrast, the costs are shared by both sides, and the collateral damage can be global, drawing the ire of countries distant from the battlefield.

Managing a problem through sanctions can contain it and contribute, along with other measures, to victory in the long run. During the Cold War, U.S. leaders understood that any military attack on the Soviet Union would trigger a ruinous nuclear war. Instead, they managed the Soviet threat for generations with a strategy of containment, which relied heavily on sanctions that blocked exports—the Soviet economy’s means of earning hard currency—and starved the Communist bloc of high-tech imports. The United States established the Coordinating Committee for Multilateral Export Controls for this purpose in 1949, together with Belgium, France, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, and the United Kingdom. Australia, Canada, Denmark, Germany, Greece, Japan, Norway, Portugal, Spain, and Turkey would eventually join. To enforce the controls, the 1951 Mutual Assistance Control Act authorized the cutoff of U.S. economic and military aid to violators. East–West trade plummeted. After the Korean War, Washington reluctantly bowed to European pressure to relax some restrictions. But after détente failed, the Carter and Reagan administrations again limited high-tech exports and tried to block Soviet sales of gas.

The Soviet Union could never overcome the economic damage from these measures. Sanctions greatly exacerbated the bloc’s preexisting economic weaknesses, such as its prioritization of heavy industry over consumer goods, its isolation from the free exchange of ideas, and its inefficient central planning based on mountains of inaccurate statistics. Soviet residents were left queuing up for subpar consumer goods. By the 1980s, new Soviet leadership concluded that their system could never close the growing economic, technological, and military gap between East and West, and they called off the Cold War.

Economic pressure by no means assures victory. But the alternative, war, rarely works as intended. In Iran today, U.S. leaders are learning that big ships and fast planes can close but not open narrow seas. A war ends when both sides agree it is over, so Iran can prolong the conflict even if the United States wants out. And even if the fighting ends soon, none of the prospective deals the Trump administration has announced to eliminate Iran’s nuclear program improve on the terms of the 2015 nuclear agreement. The now defunct memorandum of understanding that Washington and Tehran signed in June promised to lift sanctions and funnel billions to Iran in return for Iran’s reopening the Strait of Hormuz, possibly with Iranian tollbooths. Before the war, freedom of navigation through the strait was not in question.

There are two ways to end conflicts: either remove the capacity to resist or remove the will. Both sanctions and military action diminish an adversary’s ability to resist, but the bloodshed of war makes it more likely than sanctions to harden will. War, therefore, can make the underlying problem even more difficult to resolve. The cases of Iraq in 2003 and Iran in 2026 show that there was a better way. Sanctions alone would not have ended either threat, but even at their least effective, they proved useful at managing both situations, and they did so at a lower cost. Rather than a silver bullet, sanctions should be understood as a form of diplomatic chemotherapy to shrink the tumor and thereby diminish the threat. Sometimes that is the best option.

#5998. AOC campaign memo frames growth from progressive firebrand to national Democratic Party force

‘The Five’ co-hosts discuss reports that Democrats running in 2026 midterm races in swing states are asking Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., to stay away from their campaigns.

An internal strategy memo released by the campaign committee for Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-N. Y.) and distributed to key allies makes the case that the lawmaker has evolved from a progressive firebrand into a formidable national political force.

The five-page document makes no mention of a potential 2028 presidential or Senate run. Instead, it urges stakeholders to view her record as that of one of the Democratic Party's most effective messengers for working-class voters.

Following President Donald Trump’s 2024 election victory, the memo states, Ocasio-Cortez launched an aggressive nationwide push to rebuild the party around economic populism and establish a broad movement for working families.

CARVILLE TORCHES DEMOCRATIC SOCIALISTS AS 'PACK OF FOOLS,' PRAISES AOC'S POLITICAL EVOLUTION

"Over the past two years, Rep. Ocasio-Cortez has built a record of success that extends beyond her own electoral victories," the memo reads. "The Congresswoman has emerged as one of the most powerful endorsements in the United States and New York State specifically. Candidates from rural districts and battleground seats see her as an asset for their general election campaigns."

Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez at the New York Is Not For Sale rally on Oct. 26, 2025. A memo circulated among key allies highlighted the progressive lawmaker's ability to fundraise and deliver race-changing endorsements. (Neil Constantine/NurPhoto)

The document highlights her fundraising reach, citing more than 2 million contributions totaling $38 million, an average donation of $17. 56, and over $3 million funneled to other Democratic candidates and state parties.

'SQUAD' RAKES IN RECORD $2.35M WHILE SLAMMING THE DOOR ON COP DONATIONS

Her small fundraising operation is unmatched in the House and rivals that of Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., the memo claims.

According to the document, Ocasio-Cortez’s strategy was shaped by post-2024 conversations with "Trump-AOC" split-ticket voters in her New York district, who expressed deep, cross-partisan frustration with a political establishment perceived as favoring corporate billionaires over ordinary workers.

BERNIE SANDERS ADMITS DEMOCRATS FAILED WORKING-CLASS VOTERS ON ECONOMICS, DRIVING THEM TO DONALD TRUMP

"As Democrats tried to understand why Trump had won again, Rep. Ocasio-Cortez argued that the path for the party to win back working class voters should be centered on economic populism, confronting concentrated wealth and power, and revitalizing American democracy," the memo states.

U.S. Sen Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., and Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., hold a press conference to announce the Artificial Intelligence Data Center Moratorium Act at the U.S. Capitol on March 25, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Tasos Katopodis/Getty Images)

The memo points to race-altering endorsements in key primary and general election races across California, Michigan, Pennsylvania, and Montana. It highlights Abdul El-Sayed’s Senate campaign in Michigan and Sam Forstag’s Congressional primary victory in Montana as central case studies of her influence in rural and conservative-leaning areas.

AOC SIDES WITH BERNIE-BACKED PROGRESSIVE IN SENATE PRIMARY CLASH WITH SCHUMER ESTABLISHMENT

Forstag trailed significantly in the polls before joining Ocasio-Cortez and Sanders on stage at their "Fighting Oligarchy" rally in Missoula, Montana. Following her endorsement and appearance, he was able to erase the deficit and win the Democratic primary.

Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez Addresses A Crowd Of 36,000 People At The Fighting Oligarchy Rally At Gloria Molina Grand Park In Downtown Los Angeles On April 12, 2025. (Sam Ghazi / Middle East Images / Middle East Images via AFP)

The document outlines a turning point following her unsuccessful bid for ranking member of the House Oversight Committee, alongside a March 2025 budget standoff in which Senate Democratic leadership accepted concessions during federal shutdown negotiations.

In response, Ocasio-Cortez shifted focus to taking her message directly to voters.

BERNIE SANDERS DISMISSES HALEY STEVENS, SAYS MICHIGAN RACE IS AGAINST THE 'BILLIONAIRE CLASS'

Speaking at a rally in Idaho, Ocasio-Cortez laid out the central thesis of her national tour:

"I want us all to understand that this constant pressure we feel in our lives—of the water rising up to our throats, the impossibility to afford anything easily, the fear of speaking up or being who we are in public, the bitter divisions driven more by online algorithms than the truth, the crumbling of our rights and protections— understand that this, right now, is what it feels like to be governed by billionaires," she said.

In the two years since, the memo states, Ocasio-Cortez has held large-scale rallies in purple, red, and blue places alike.

"She made race-changing endorsements in swing districts and deep-blue party primaries, as well as in her home base of New York City," the memo added.

#5995. Election expert confident about voting today, more concerned about what happens next

Voters cast their ballots in the midterm elections Tuesday morning at the Brooklyn Museum in New York. Timothy A. Clary/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

As Americans cast ballots in Tuesday's midterm elections, an election security expert says he's more concerned about what happens after polls close than about disruptions during voting.

David Becker, a former Justice Department voting rights lawyer and founder of the nonpartisan Center for Election Innovation and Research, told Morning Edition that nearly 60 million Americans have already voted with hardly any problems.

His comments come as voting rights and immigrant advocacy groups prepare for the possibility of federal agents near polling places, despite assurances from the Trump administration that it has no plans to deploy them. In Arizona, one organization has trained more than 100 community members to monitor potential irregularities outside voting sites.

Becker pointed to a federal law, 18 U.S.C. 592, that restricts certain deployments of armed personnel near polling places. Violations can carry penalties of up to five years in prison and disqualification from federal office. He said courts would intervene if immigration agents or troops were unlawfully deployed.

But he cautioned against overreaction in the news media and speculation online. Images of officers near polling places, stripped of context, could spread unnecessary alarm and discourage voters from casting their ballots.

Election officials have prepared for more routine disruptions, including traffic jams and bad weather. Most voters are unlikely to face long waits, according to Becker.

"I think we expect to hear statements from losing candidates and losing parties that the election was rigged when we know, of course, that our elections are secure and verified," Becker said.

During his conversation with NPR's Steve Inskeep, Becker discussed what voters might encounter on Election Day and why he's more concerned about what comes next.

#5985. A new cold war: hackers target fridges across the globe

A woman walks past empty fresh-food refrigerators at an Intermarché supermarket in Névez, western France, on Oct. 12, following a cyberattack affecting the French supermarket chains Intermarché and Cooperative U that caused their refrigerators to defrost. Some 150 to 200 Intermarché stores were targeted in the attack on Oct. 12, along with nearly 100 Coopérative U outlets, the retailers said. Fred Tanneau/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

It was a May morning in Israel when employees at a large food production plant watched in horror as the office printers started spontaneously spitting out reams of threatening messages.

Then, employees found malicious code on corporate computers designed to wipe away internal data.

Finally, an engineer smelled smoke coming out of the industrial freezers, only to find broken compressors and badly spoiled food inside.

The company's operations ground to a halt for over a week while engineers waited for new parts to arrive from abroad.

It was all the result of a targeted, multi-pronged cyberattack.

"I do a lot of cyber incidents, but this is the kind of incident that I know will leave a mark on the people in charge," said Omri Segev Moyal, head of an Israeli incident response firm called Profero, which responded to the attack.

Based on forensic data and direct claims from the culprits, Moyal is confident Iranian military hackers who currently go by the name Cyber Isnaad Front, the same group linked to influence campaigns targeting U.S. elections in 2024, are behind the attack on the refrigeration systems. It was a destructive digital strike that took place during the waning days of a ceasefire between Iran and Israel when tensions were ratcheting up.

In the months since, it appears to have become a trend.

Still unidentified hackers have targeted refrigeration controllers around the world. They have spoiled fresh produce and cost businesses time and money. These attacks have taken place on U.S. military bases in August, French supermarkets in October, and at food stores from small markets to big box stores like IKEA in countries including Sweden, Estonia, Ireland, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, and Belgium in early November.

"We're observing targeted attacks gradually built and practiced against Israel, tested in Europe and the U.S., and unleashed [now]," Moyal said in an interview with NPR.

Across the globe, bad actors with access to AI are breaking into and taking control of the aging machines that run global critical infrastructure, from water pumps to cooling systems. That should put pressure on governments and companies to find solutions to long-standing security challenges before things get even worse, cybersecurity experts say.

"With great connectivity comes great responsibility," said Josh Corman, a former cybersecurity official with the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency who specializes in public safety. "As we connect industrial refrigeration to the Internet, we expose it to accidents and adversaries," Corman continued. "While these [attacks] hit food," he said, the next ones could go after "temperature sensitive drugs and laboratory materials."

As early as March, hackers appear to have targeted a small chain of kosher grocery stores in Israel, according to a video posted to social media. In the video, a customer is touching previously frozen food including soft, squishy packages of meat that have clearly spoiled. The company did not immediately respond to a request for comment about the incident.

A couple months later, hackers went after a bigger target in the food production plant and its associated businesses, the incident Profero was called in to respond to. Moyal declined to share the name of the company due to client confidentiality, but described further details about the incident to NPR.

The hackers broke into the industrial controllers that helped manage and monitor the freezers. They also adjusted the safety limits, opened the gas pressure valves, changed the credentials, disabled alerts, and destroyed forensic evidence in the middle of the night. They took advantage of the vulnerable devices as well as the laws of thermodynamics to render the compressors "eliminated," Moyal said. "They were blown up … not like C4 exploding, but they got overused and there was smoke. They were not repairable."

The technical sophistication of these attacks is advancing, in part thanks to AI, Moyal added.

"What we were able to see is they very rapidly were able to, almost like magic, go from initially playing with the system to fully understanding how the configuration works," Moyal said. "They tried the simple things … and then they started to bombard the system with different types of configurations until they destroyed the compressor. It wasn't human, the amount of configurations they were able to try in a short time span."

Replacing parts and recovering from the attack were costly, in time and money, Moyal added.

A propaganda video about the Israeli grocery store hack was released in October. While it was largely wiped from the internet, it was shared with NPR on the condition that the identity of Moyal's client wouldn't be revealed. NPR is choosing not to publish it for that reason and not to aid in the spread of propaganda. That video suggests stoking fear was part of the hackers' motivation.

In the video, labeled with a glowing red triangle embossed with the "Cyber Isnaad Front" logo, a masked man sits in a room full of digital screens, typing away on a keyboard before revealing hacked surveillance footage of customers across Israel in supermarkets that were affected by the large-scale destruction, as well as employees at the company finding the printed propaganda materials.

In Arabic, the hackers claim to have launched the attack on behalf of the starving children of Gaza, closing out with the hacker saluting French students in French, likely a reference to ongoing riots in France over education. It's unclear why the video references French students, though the group has impersonated a French far-right group in the past and targeted the Israeli delegation to the 2024 Paris Olympics. The video was also released just weeks before the widespread digital attacks against French supermarkets, but it's unclear if that timing is coincidental.

Ari Ben Am, the co-founder of a threat intelligence company called Telemetry Data Labs that tracks chatter on the social media platform Telegram, has been tracking this group for years. He says it's working on behalf of the Iranian military.

Ben Am, an adjunct fellow at the Foundation for the Defense of Democracy's Center on Cyber and Technology Innovation, says it's one of the more technically capable Iranian hacking groups he has seen.

"They appear to have a higher level of investment, a higher quality of activity like actual hacking…they really do much more effective, more holistic operations," he said.

Ben Am conducted an early analysis of the technical details of the hack of the Israeli food plant shared by Profero. He concluded that it's possible that these hackers may have shared some digital tools like wiper malware with other Iranian hacking groups like the Cyber Av3ngers. That group hacked machines in water and wastewater treatment facilities in the U.S. in 2023 to display propaganda messages.

Cyber Isnaad Front, Ben Am says, has a history of hacking into surveillance cameras and promoting their activities across a range of social media platforms, and it's the only Iranian group he has seen use human actors in videos.

"They're capable of consistently compromising actual organizations that have some degree of cyber defense," he concluded.

In August, there was what the Pentagon described as a "possible refrigeration disruption" at multiple U.S. military commissaries, grocery stores owned and operated by the Defense Commissary Agency, a wing of the Pentagon. It's unclear exactly how many experienced outages, though an independent blogger cited social media posts and independent confirmations of disruptions for at least nine locations. Very few details have emerged about how bad actors might have compromised those systems.

The Pentagon has so far refused to officially acknowledge whether or not it believes hackers were responsible for the refrigeration disruptions.

However, the Defense Criminal Investigative Service, the criminal investigative arm of the Pentagon's Office of Inspector General, has partnered with the Army Criminal Investigation Division's Cyber Field Office and French law enforcement following the hacks across stores in France, according to a statement shared with NPR.

According to two former officials who requested anonymity to discuss an ongoing investigation, investigators are treating the outages as a cyberattack and believe some evidence points towards Iran as the culprit, though there's not high confidence in that attribution yet. That suspicion was first reported by NextGov.

While many questions remain about the incidents at the commissaries, there are clues across the internet about vulnerabilities that may have exposed these systems to compromise.

Publicly, there are indications that the Defense Commissary agency deploys remote monitoring control tools for their refrigeration and HVAC systems that could be vulnerable to ongoing attacks.

According to a review of public government contracting documents by NPR, multiple U.S. Defense Commissary installations, including several which have disclosed outages like Fort Irwin and Fort Huachuca, are operating Danfoss refrigeration remote control systems, products that have been identified by French officials and cybersecurity experts from a firm called Claroty as being vulnerable to attack.

French officials have cited those same Danfoss machines as one of the vectors of the confirmed incidents across French supermarkets in October.

According to those same contracting documents, the U.S. Defense Commissary agency is using several older versions of the Danfoss controllers, including some from the mid-2000s that can no longer be updated with new, secure software.

While a spokesperson for Danfoss declined to comment on specific customer relationships, she told NPR that the company is "aware of incidents in France affecting a limited number of refrigeration management systems" and "are in close contact with affected customers to support them in addressing their situation."

Danfoss has advised customers to mitigate the risks by installing the latest firmware, removing the devices from the internet, and operating behind a firewall and VPN, Tate explained.

Social media posts about individual incidents on U.S. bases suggest these were not isolated outages that might've been caused by other environmental factors.

Fort Huachuca, an Army base in Arizona that houses the Army's intelligence division, shared on Facebook that the post's commissary experienced "an overnight equipment failure" that resulted in "all freezers [going into] defrost mode." The page's moderator clarified in the comments section of the post that there was no power outage, and that the switch to defrost mode "heated up all the food."

"Trust us when we say it was 'spoiled,'" the account posted.

The official social media account for Fort Irwin, the home of the Army's National Training Center in the Mojave Desert in California, shared that technicians were "working around the clock" to "repair" the systems, and that the outages were affecting bases around the country. They wrote that they were waiting for "temperatures [to] stabilize" before restocking.

Ashley Upshaw, a military spouse living on Fort Irwin, told NPR she found out about the outage online through social media and a spouse forum.

"People were posting that all the frozen areas were blocked off, that there were no fruits, no vegetables. Like everything was cleared out. We didn't know exactly what was going on," she said.

People on base, including those in town for training, were forced to drive more than 40 minutes to go to the nearest grocery store, buy shelf-stable foods from the base's thrift store, or pool resources with neighbors, she said. "There were a lot of people helping others out," she said.

When asked what the military told families about the incident, she said she didn't believe there was any official announcement about a cyberattack but that was what people were speculating about on social media, including on a popular page called "U.S. Army W.T.F! Moments."

Across social media, commenters expressed frustration that the commissary refrigeration systems were connected to the internet at all.

"You need clearance to even get on base. So why not have the same mentality for other things like refrigeration systems and stuff like that?" Upshaw said.

Following the mysterious outages at the bases, things were quiet for weeks. The disruptions were limited to a handful of targets. It's unclear why hackers didn't manage to compromise more of the 107 Defense Commissary stores in the U.S. and abroad.

Then, in October, a manager of a French supermarket outside of Dijon told NPR he got a call from a surveillance company at four in the morning telling him that temperatures were shooting up in the store's refrigerators and cold rooms before he came to find his freezers practically turned into ovens.

His store was one of hundreds whose fresh food was spoiled that day. The French cybersecurity agency ANSSI is in the middle of an ongoing investigation into what happened, and is in communication not just with the supermarkets and the companies that make the controllers, but also with other industrial sectors "which might be impacted by this incident," a press officer for the ANSSI wrote in a statement to NPR. The attacks on French supermarkets were more wide ranging, hitting hundreds of supermarkets across multiple chains including Intermarché, Coopérative U and Carrefour. Even so, the number of affected systems was actually still a small fraction of the total number of stores and devices within those company's portfolios.

Now, with attacks hitting supermarkets in at least six more European countries, the trend of compromising refrigeration technology appears to be continuing to escalate. Some officials including those in Sweden and Estonia have said impacts were more limited than they could have been, given the warnings they received from what happened in France. However, the full extent of the impacts of the new wave of attacks is still unfolding.

Officials are still investigating all these separate outages, while governments and industries are scrambling to determine whether they're still operating devices that are vulnerable.

Hackers remotely dialing up the temperature in industrial freezers or damaging other critical devices from a world away sounds a lot like science fiction.

But it's happening. Despite a brief interlude years ago when some criminal hackers claimed that they wouldn't target sensitive systems like those used in hospitals, many hackers have since done so.

That's likely because the opportunity to make money by holding critical systems hostage is simply too lucrative to ignore, like the ransomware attack on Colonial Pipeline in 2021.

Meanwhile, these kinds of attacks are increasingly becoming a normal part of geopolitical conflict, whether that's Russian hackers shutting off the power in Ukraine, or Chinese hackers burrowing into U.S. critical infrastructure to provide a foothold to cause damage in the event of a potential future conflict like a Chinese invasion of Taiwan. Additionally, experts have linked a series of disruptive attacks on U.S. water treatment plants in August to Iran, within weeks of the Defense Commissary outages.

In reality, securing operational technology, the digital machines that control physical processes in the real world, is a uniquely difficult challenge that has plagued cybersecurity experts focused on it for many years and is only getting worse.

"This has always been an issue," explained Michael Garcia, the Policy Director of the Operational Technology Cybersecurity Coalition, a coalition of government and industry partners working to improve cybersecurity and resilience for critical infrastructure. "We know the threat is there, and it's just a matter of time before a bad actor compromises something."

It's difficult to estimate the full extent of how many of these devices, from remote monitoring systems to what are called programmable logic controllers, are vulnerable to attack and for what reason, though cybersecurity firms have identified thousands of refrigeration systems alone still exposed to basic attacks even after the widely publicized incidents in France.

In recent years, there's been a trend towards greater connectivity between devices, to allow individuals to remotely manage massive networks of machines from a central location. It makes sense, particularly for major corporations who are dealing with complex machines that can't be adequately managed by individuals onsite, unlike a household refrigerator. But many of these systems were not originally designed with internet connectivity or security in mind.

On top of that, many of these systems are aging and can no longer be patched at all, because the companies that made them have moved on to newer versions.

And finally, many of the officials or executives whose agencies or businesses run operational technology have limited to no visibility into the specific devices they're using, which ones are vulnerable, or whether or not they have cybersecurity protections in place. Some of those people haven't invested in securing these systems the way they should.

"Internet exposed devices were always the soft underbelly of operational technology," explained Amir Preminger, the chief technology officer for cybersecurity firm Claroty. Claroty first published a piece on the vulnerabilities in refrigeration technology back in August.

"Devices can be vulnerable to [bugs] that were discovered and nobody fixed them," Preminger said. "They can be known vulnerabilities and nobody patched. And there can just be the fact that there's no security measures. If you leave that door open, anyone can come in and do whatever he wants."

Eleanor Beardsley and Daniel Estrin contributed to this story.

#5980. It's Election Day. Control of Congress is on the line, and what else you need to know

A voter walks to a polling site in Alpharetta, Ga., on Tuesday. Ryan Murphy/AP hide caption

📲 Get the latest on the midterms & more in the NPR AppVote counting starts tonight. Follow along as polls close — and tune in to our live special starting at 8 p.m. ET.• Hour-by-hour guide: How to watch election night• 6 things to watch for to see which party might control Congress• Be patient! It's going to take a while to know who won in some races

It's finally here. Election Day. Today, voting officially ends.

No more talk of polls. Now, it's about results.

More than 59 million people have already cast ballots. That's nearly half of the total ballots cast in the last midterm election. Democrats hold a double-digit advantage going into today, based on available data from early voting.

But a major key today will be how many Republicans show up to the polls. There are so many red states and districts in play, and the GOP votes overwhelmingly in person on Election Day.

Democrats are favored to win the House. They only have to flip three seats for control, and the landscape has shifted considerably, even in the past month, in favor of Democrats.

Republicans tried to insulate themselves with rare, mid-decade redistricting. But it likely won't be enough to help the party keep the chamber.

Voters cast their ballots at the Meadows Mall in Las Vegas on Tuesday. Ethan Miller/Getty Images hide caption

In the Senate, the outcome is anyone's guess. Democrats need to flip four seats to gain control of the Senate, something that was unthinkable at the beginning of this cycle. But strong Democratic recruits and a difficult national political environment for Republicans have made it a possibility.

Democrats are favored to flip one seat — North Carolina. But beyond that, the races are either toss-ups or Republicans are slight favorites. And the elections are taking place on GOP turf. Apart from Maine, the five toss-ups held by Republicans are in states Trump won by double digits in 2024.

That's why Trump has been in so many red states ahead of Election Day to try and turn out his base. Republicans' top target is Michigan. If they win that, Democrats' calculus changes.

High Republican turnout could limit Democrats to one pick up. Low turnout among Republican voters could see a wipeout of up to six or seven seats — and result in Democratic control of the Senate.

There are a lot of variables for how things will go today. Results will start to come in at 6 p.m. ET in Kentucky and Indiana, and then it will be off to the races. And while everyone wants to know the results on election night, in many places the results will not be known. So patience is key as states count votes in what are expected to be many close races.

It could take days. It could take weeks, but ballots are being counted and results are coming soon.

#5970. How NPR makes race calls in the 2026 election

Voters enter a polling station at Midtown Mall on October 31, 2026 in Anchorage, Alaska. Justin Sullivan/Getty Images North America hide caption

How NPR makes race calls in the 2026 election

Voters are voting, ballots are being counted and results are starting to come in for 435 House races, 35 Senate races and 36 gubernatorial contests as well as ballot measures and thousands of state legislative seats. But how will NPR call races on election night? Determining a winner in any of the many races across the country is a big job. That's why NPR partners with the Associated Press — and has for many years — to ensure that races are called accurately.

The Associated Press has been calling races since 1848 and even now, nearly 180 years later, the process for making those calls is pretty simple. Here's how the AP explains its race calling process:

Race callers collaborate with analysts who focus on statewide races, such as those for U.S. Senate and governor, and elections for the U.S. House of Representatives. The decision editors on AP's Decision Team sign off on every race call for president, U.S. Senate and governor, as well as races of significance for U.S. House.

Together, they are looking at far more than just the overall vote totals. They study the incoming vote county by county. In states where the information is available, they look at the vote by type of ballot: cast in person at polling places, or in advance by mail or in person. They are also in constant contact with AP's vote count team, in search of the latest information about what's been counted so far and how many ballots may still be left to count.

Once AP has an idea of how many ballots are outstanding versus how many have been counted, their staff can start to tabulate the likelihood of a certain winner being victorious in a certain contest based on a lot of different factors: where the ballots that are outstanding are coming from, what the makeup of those communities looks like and even, how the individual chose to vote.

For NPR's part, once the AP calls a race, we will alert our broadcast and digital audiences on air as part of special coverage and online through digital stories. We also ensure that our hundreds of member stations also have access to that information. We will use AP's data to populate our election results pages so viewers can keep track, in real time.

How are races called when not all votes have been counted?

But sometimes, races might be called without all — or even most — of the vote counted. How is that possible?

The Associated Press explained in 2024 that it uses that same calculation of ballots, external factors and other data to inform its decisions.

In almost all cases, races can be called well before 100% of the votes have been counted. The AP's team of election journalists and analysts will call a race as soon as a clear winner can be determined. That may sound obvious, but it is the guiding principle that drives the organization's election race-calling process.

The AP's race calls are not predictions and are not based on speculation. They are declarations based on an analysis of vote results and other election data that one candidate has emerged as the winner and that no other candidate in the race will be able to overtake the winner once all the votes have been counted.

It sounds simple, right? The AP won't call races until "a clear winner can be determined." But that doesn't always require all of the votes for every precinct for every race. Sometimes it's all about the math: can this candidate feasibly win with the votes outstanding in the places they are yet to be counted? And that raises another, really important point.

The AP – and therefore NPR – will not project races. The team at the AP will wait until it has certainty to call raises and that is why its race calls are trusted by hundreds of news organizations, including NPR. Because they wait until there is surety, even if it means waiting just a bit longer to be sure.

On an ethical note, NPR will not make race calls just because a candidate declares victory, nor will NPR use a concession speech or statement to make a race call either. That is true of the Associated Press as well:

AP does not make projections or name apparent or likely winners. If our race callers cannot definitively say a candidate has won, we do not engage in speculation. For example, AP did not call the closely contested race in 2000 between George W. Bush and Al Gore – we stood behind our assessment that the margin in Florida made it too close to say who won.

AP only calls a race when we are fully confident that race has been won – defined most simply as the moment the trailing candidates no longer have a path to victory.

When AP makes a race call, we back it up with transparent reporting into the research and analysis that led us to say with certainty who has won. This explanatory journalism is as much a hallmark of our coverage of U.S. elections as the race calls themselves.

While election night is the start of these race calls, it likely will not be the end. Counting voters can take a while. Race calls will be tabulated to determine the balance of power in the Senate and the House of Representatives. NPR will cover all major race calls and provide ongoing coverage on air and online of just how the 2026 election shakes out.

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

#5961. From 'model minority' to target, anti-Indian hate on the rise

A security vehicle is parked outside of Sri Venkateswara Swami (Balaji) Temple in Aurora, Ill., on Oct. 15, 2026. Laura Bargfeld/AP hide caption

A 19-year-old man's alleged plot to carry out a mass shooting at a Hindu temple in suburban Chicago last month is drawing attention to a recent and steep rise in anti-South Asian and anti-Hindu hate. Police in Palatine, Ill., announced that on Oct. 11, a college student and Taco Bell cashier named Alexandros Paporakis tried to carry out the attack at the Sri Venkateswara Swami Temple in Aurora, Ill., roughly 40 miles west of Chicago. They said the plot was averted by a jammed gun.

"Something of this scale … has not been directed at South Asians before," said Rohit Chopra, professor of communications at Santa Clara University and visiting fellow at the Center for South Asia at Stanford University.

Two decades ago, the predominant narrative about Indian immigrants to the U.S. was glowing, describing the relatively small population as a "model minority." But Chopra says that over the last four years, a combination of growing anti-immigrant populism, economic anxiety and rising political visibility have dramatically changed the script. Today, he said, online and political discourse more often depict Indian Americans and a broader community of people of South Asian descent in conspiracist, degrading and dehumanizing ways.

"It's become a question of who is American, and deliberately trying to keep the Hindu- and Indian-American community out of those conversations entirely with these sort of preconceived notions and bias against the community," said Prasiddha Sudhakar, a board member of the Network Contagion Research Institute. Sudhakar was formerly a researcher at the NCRI who coauthored some of that organization's reports on the topic.

Most concerning, said Sudhakar and Chopra, is that the toxic online rhetoric they have documented has moved from fringe social media platforms to mainstream ones in a short period of time. And, they said, they see strong connections between online hate and offline violence.

"It didn't start off as mainstream, maybe even like a year or a few years ago. You still saw it on the internet," Sudhakar said. "But what has actually become more important now is that this is reaching many different parts of society in a scale that we've never seen before."

Paporakis has been charged in Illinois with making a terrorist threat and unlawful possession of a rifle, both felonies. Police said his coworkers called to report the attempted massacre. One of them, according to an affidavit filed by a Cook County assistant state attorney, had spoken by phone with the defendant shortly after the attempted attack. The affidavit further states that Paporakis had also sent that coworker messages, including an eight-page document he had written outlining his motivation for the alleged plot.

Later that day, police said that a search of Paporakis' apartment turned up a bag containing an AR-style rifle, 300 rounds of ammunition, eight loaded magazines, one loaded extended magazine and body armor. They also found a gas can and items bearing Nazi symbols and anti-Indian slurs.

"The seriousness of this alleged threat cannot be overstated," said Palatine Police Chief William Nord at a press conference.

Court filings state that the written document was filled with "derogatory and racially charged slurs" directed at "people of Indian ancestry, Jews and African Americans," and that it described those groups as "subhumans." It also indicated that the defendant had settled on the temple location two months prior, but had been planning an attack for two years. In the writings, the defendant allegedly described himself as "a sociopathic white supremacist Neo Nazi filled with hatred, fantasizing about mass murder nearly every day."

The day that the defendant allegedly went to the temple coincided with the beginning of a major Hindu nine-day festival. Among the many in attendance were U.S. Congressman Raja Krishnamoorthi , D-Ill., and his family. Krishnamoorthi has since called for the FBI to investigate the incident as a federal hate crime.

Sudhakar, whose research was published through the NCRI, said that fringe social media spaces such as 4chan and Gab were the original loci of denigrating and dehumanizing rhetoric about Indians and South Asians. But around 2022, Sudhakar said, her research started to see some of the negative attitudes morph into full-blown conspiracy theories. One of them was a baseless theory about a "Brahmin Organized Government."

"This is the idea of, oh, there's like this elitist group of people that are of high class, high privilege that are sort of occupying the entire government, that this is all like some sort of secret ploy," Sudhakar said.

The term echoes the conspiracy theory of the "Zionist Organized Government," or "ZOG," widely espoused by neo-Nazis. This baseless narrative posits that a cabal of powerful Jews runs global affairs, and closely ties with the Great Replacement Theory. It claims that Jewish puppetmasters are deliberately diluting the power of white Christians by promoting immigration, interracial relations and diversity.

Sudhakar said the main distinction is that the anti-Indian conspiracy theories center more heavily on fears over immigration and jobs, rather than changing demographics. Just as the narratives position people of Indian origin as all-powerful, they also invoke dehumanizing tropes about Indians as "dirty" immigrant "invaders" being brought to the U.S. to displace technology sector workers.

"What we saw was that what began as sort of this anti-Hindu meme that began on 4chan started moving into these mainstream platforms [like X]," she said. "And every time that you'd see Indians or anyone of Hindu origin kind of gain prominence, that these same code words would also be increasing."

Sudhakar said those spikes coincided with events such as Parag Agarwal's appointment as CEO of Twitter, Sriram Krishnan's selection as former White House policy adviser on Artificial Intelligence and the installation of Usha Vance as second lady.

But 2025, said Sudhakar, was when online anti-Hindu and anti-Indian hate truly mushroomed. Chopra, the professor at Santa Clara University, found the same, in research co-authored for the Center for the Study of Organized Hate. It found that between January 2025 and June 2026, anti-South Asian hate speech on the social media platform X was 240% higher than it was in January 2024.

"I think the right has very effectively succeeded in making the H-1B visa issue a political hot button," Chopra said, noting that Indian nationals account for more than 70% of H-1B visas. "Every time there was some development to do with the H-1B visas or a remark that might be made by a figure in authority, you'd find a spike in hate online on X."

Since the alleged temple massacre was disclosed to the public, calls are growing within the Hindu and Indian communities in the U.S. to speak more vocally about racism. Congressman Krishnamoorthy released a video urging the community to become more engaged. Chopra said that in some ways, the rapid uptick in anti-South Asian hate online has been possible because there has been little organized opposition.

"The South Asian community is particularly vulnerable for, I think, a number of reasons. One is this combination of high median income of certain groups of visible success … combined with the fact that it does not have significant political power," Chopra said.

The rise of Indian Americans on the political stage in recent years has been significant, given that estimates put the community's population only around 5 million. These figures include Usha Vance, FBI Director Kash Patel, former Director of National Security Tulsi Gabbard, Ohio GOP gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy and former GOP presidential candidate Nikki Hayley. But Chopra said that these milestones do not equate to the political power of an organized constituency.

"It doesn't have the kind of political power that can … significantly shift elections, Chopra said.

Additionally, explicit racism directed at South Asians has become disturbingly evident within the political arena, Chopra said. In September, a Republican candidate for statewide office posted a screenshot to X of cheering University of Texas football fans after a team victory. It featured a crowd of revelers that included many of South Asian and Asian origin, with the candidate's comment that "social media is exposing how many Americans have been displaced by foreigners. It's everywhere and far worse than any of us could imagine."

The Department of Homeland Security also set off controversy when it shared an image of a poster captioned "Transformers," with an image of a Sikh man and the words "Get off our roads, you don't know how to drive Mr. Singh." The message was understood to refer to an August 25 tragedy, in which a commercial truck driver of Indian nationality illegally made a U-turn on a Florida highway, and killed three people. After a backlash, DHS took the post down.

But while calls are mounting for the community to organize, there are unique challenges. For one, said Pushpita Prasad of the Coalition of Hindus of North America, Hindu temples do not serve quite the same function as houses of worship do in many other religions.

"We don't belong to temples and we are not part of congregations," Prasad said. "So for us, organizing and finding our voice and sharing information is that much harder."

Additionally, Prasad noted that the economic and professional success of many people of Indian origin in the U.S. masks the uncertainty many may feel about their long-term status in the country. According to the Pew Research Center, two-thirds of the Indian population in the U.S. are immigrants, and of those, nearly half are not naturalized citizens.

"If you happen to come from India, you will find that a lot of Hindus who may be very well off have a great job, seem very well established, they're not really able to speak because they're in that weird sort of immigration limbo," Prasad said. "So that's another reason why a community that seems well off, at least in media chatter, is not as vocal or as organized as it probably should have been."

#5954. Distributed Energy Infrastructure completes New York solar project despite site challenges

Distributed Energy Infrastructure (DEI), an EPC firm for solar and battery storage, has completed the 3.37-MWDC Sapphire Solar project in Jefferson County, New York.

The Sapphire Solar project. Credit: Distributed Energy Infrastructure

Shallow bedrock and a high water table presented site challenges that required the team to adapt its design mid-construction. During excavation, DEI encountered shale bedrock only 16 to 20 in. below grade across several areas of the site, accompanied by groundwater continuously seeping into open trenches. The conditions impacted installation and equipment pad areas, making the original underground DC conduit design impractical to implement.

DEI’s engineering, construction, procurement and contractor teams collaborated closely to quickly develop a revised design that converted a portion of the DC underground scope to an above-ground CAB system. Underground trenching was retained at row-to-row crossings and other locations where it remained appropriate. The revised design also preserved site access for the owner’s long-term operations and maintenance needs.

The full redesign, material procurement and field implementation were completed within the timeframe originally planned for the underground trenching scope.

“When we encounter unexpected challenges, our goal is to address them quickly and adapt our approach to what will work best for the site,” said Rusfid Haroon, senior project manager at DEI. “Even hitting shallow bedrock and water table across the site, we brought together the experience of each team working on the site to re-evaluate, re-design and implement a solution that kept the project moving while ensuring long-term reliability.”

News item from Distributed Energy Infrastructure

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5950. 'Messy,' 'divided' and 'scary': swing voters rate the country ahead of Election Day

Voters Lee, Wally, Theresa, and Gerald. Tara Anand for NPR hide caption

Gerald and Wally both voted for President Trump in 2024, helping him with the state of Georgia. Their votes in 2026 illustrate the challenges the president and his party face in today's midterms.

Gerald is still all in, giving Trump an A+++. He cast his ballot on the first day of early voting in Georgia for Republicans Mike Collins for Senate and Rick Jackson for governor.

"Red all the way down the ballot," Gerald said. "There was one instance where there was nothing but a Democrat running unopposed and I preferred to write my own name in, because I refuse to give a Democrat my vote."

Wally, on the other hand, represents a fraying of the Trump coalition. Every time he's been asked since March, he's given the president an F.

"I just don't think that he's an effective leader and I thought that as Americans we'd do so much better," he said.

Wally said inflation is the top issue influencing his vote, but he doesn't have a lot of hope that any one politician or any one election can fix it. He also voted early in Georgia for the Democratic Senate incumbent Jon Ossoff and gubernatorial candidate Keisha Lance Bottoms — a vote against Trump and MAGA as much as anything else.

He gives Democrats and Republicans in Congress an F as well.

"I think these people are essentially stealing money from the American public and we need wholesale change in this midterm, and we also need a big change for the next presidential election," he said.

Wally and Gerald are among a group of swing voters in swing states NPR has been checking in with regularly as part of a long-term reporting project called Swing Shift. NPR has agreed not to use their full names so they can speak freely about politics, without fear of personal or professional repercussions.

All of the Swing Shift voters have cast a ballot for President Trump over the past decade. Most of them voted for him just two years ago.

When Americans cast their votes in the midterm elections, they'll have the chance to weigh in on the state of the country, nearly two years into President Trump's second term in office.

Asked for one word or phrase to describe how they are feeling, Swing Shift voters used words like "messy," "divided," "disarray," and "scary. "

In the week before Election Day, their assessment of Trump's performance on the job roughly aligns with national polls: most were unhappy or underwhelmed, often citing the war in Iran and high energy prices as major concerns. Only two of the 11 Swing Shift voters told NPR they were happy with Trump's second term.

Theresa, a Pennsylvania voter who voted for former President Joe Biden in 2020, then President Trump in 2024, said she would give the president a C-.

"Because he said the war was only going to be a few weeks. And we knew that was not the case," she said.

Lee from Nevada, one of the voters happy with Trump, still voiced concerns about the war with Iran. He gave Trump's performance in office this term an A-.

"Because the war in Iran is taking kind of long," he said.

When NPR first spoke to Lee, he was out of work. Now, with a new job, his outlook on the economy has improved. He said he's gotten even more Republican since the last election and more suspicious of Democrats.

Meanwhile, Quintero, a Biden to Trump voter, also from Nevada, told NPR he is swinging back to Democrats in this year's midterms.

That's the challenge for Republicans: two years in, the Trump 2024 base isn't supplying reliable Republican votes.

Even as this election, like all midterms, has turned into a referendum on the president and his policies, he isn't on the ballot. So the question is how their feelings about Trump and his presidency will translate to votes for the House, Senate and governors.

In Pennsylvania, the Swing Shift voters are divided: three out of four voted for Trump in 2024. Now, three of them plan to vote for Democratic Gov. Josh Shapiro, who is seeking re-election.

"I might get in there and change my mind again. But at this point, it is going to be with the politician that we're familiar with, which is Shapiro," Theresa said.

In the northern suburbs of Philadelphia, Stephen lives in one of the most competitive House districts in the country, Pennsylvania's 1st Congressional District. Incumbent Republican Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick has survived a Democratic wave before, and Stephen is willing to stick with him.

"I would still re-elect Brian Fitzpatrick but everything else, I would say maybe Democratic or…maybe do a write in," Stephen said.

At the top of the ticket, though, he's voting for Shapiro.

Only John from Philadelphia said he voted for the Republican in the governor's race. He said he feels like the country is "teetering" and he isn't happy with either party. He follows the news closely and sees where the political tides are headed.

"I think there will be a lot of investigations, a lot of impeachment talk and a lot of gridlock in Washington," John said of a possible Democratic win in the midterms. "And I don't know if we can afford that right now. So my word to describe the state of the country is 'anxious.'"

#5943. EIA: Renewables could overtake natural gas output in 2027

Renewable energy is still outpacing fossil fuels as the leading new sources of electricity on the U.S. grid. Electrical generation from renewable energy sources has increased by 10%, or more than 43 GW, in the first eight months of 2026, according to an analysis of Energy Information Administration (EIA) data by the SUN DAY Campaign.

Renewables are expected to account for more than 40% of the United States’ total electrical output over the next year. At the start of September, renewable energy accounted for 34.5% of utility-scale generation in the country.

Utility-scale and small-scale solar are the two fastest growing renewable energy sources being tied to the grid. Compared to the first eight months of 2025, utility-scale solar projects increased by 20%, and smaller systems increased by 12% in 2026. Between September 2025 and August 2026 utility-scale solar increased by 27.1 GW and small-scale solar 6.5-GW. Battery energy storage systems’ capacity increased by 17.6 GW.

New natural gas and nuclear power only grew by 2.6% an 1.3%. Coal’s electrical output decreased by more than 8%. If new renewable energy proliferation continues at its expected pace, EIA is predicting that it could overtake natural gas output by summer 2027.

“Each new issue of EIA’s ‘Electric Power Monthly’ reinforces the theme that the growth of solar, wind and battery storage has become unstoppable,” said Ken Bossong, executive director of the SUN DAY Campaign. “Meanwhile, the share of the nation’s electrical generation and generating capacity provided by fossil fuels and nuclear continues to shrink.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5936. Four types of House races to watch on election night to know how it might go

Voters fill out their ballot at a polling station inside Bennett Community Center American Legion 104 in Bennett, Iowa on Tuesday. Joshua Lott/Getty Images hide caption

Midterms coverage: Live results | What to know | Stories around the countryStay connected: NPR Politics Podcast | NPR Politics newsletter | NPR app

Democrats appear poised to retake control of the House on Tuesday. If they do, will their majority be built upon a blue wave or a tiny trickle? A previous NPR analysis found both parties spending deep and wide in a competitive landscape that spans 70 districts, ranging from longtime tossups to newly-competitive seats in deeply conservative areas.

Republicans came into Election Day with a narrow 218-214 seat advantage in the House and facing severe headwinds as the party in power. This midterm cycle has also seen a record number of departures from Congress, and polls show voters aren't happy about things like the economy and the ongoing war in Iran.

So what separates a good night for Democrats from a blue wave that sees dozens of House seats change hands? Here's NPR's cheat sheet to four types of House races to watch as results come in:

The first results of the night with national implications will likely be from Kentucky's 6th Congressional District (KY-06) and Indiana's 5th (IN-05), two districts where polls close first (in the 6 p.m. ET hour) and whose 2024 margins for President Trump (15 and 17 points, respectively) could act as a preview for how much voters have soured on Republicans.

Democrats and Republicans alike have spent heavily in the Kentucky race, where Democrat Zach Dembo faces Republican Ralph Alvarado, so the increased investment is worth noting as a factor on the outcome. If Democrats flip one or both of these races, then it will likely be a long night for the GOP.

Florida also counts and reports its votes quickly, and is home to more than a half dozen races that are viewed as toss-ups or slightly favor one party or the other, so as these districts have projected winners there will be more insight into other races across the country.

Like the Kentucky contest, both parties have spent significant sums in races like the open seat in FL-22 with Democrat Pia Dandiya facing off against Republican Casey Askar and incumbent Democratic Rep. Jared Moskowitz versus Republican Scott Singer in the newly-redrawn FL-25 race.

Other races to watch: FL-27, NC-11, SC-01, VA-01

In the final seven days of the midterms, a handful of House races have seen more than $10 million in ad spending, according to data from AdImpact. It's a sign of where both parties think the saying "It all comes down to turnout!" is more than a cliche. That includes districts in Michigan, such as MI-07, where progressive Democrat William Lawrence is challenging incumbent Republican Tom Barrett in a seat that Trump carried by just over one percent in 2024 and the open MI-10, where Democrat Christina Hines faces Republican Mike Bouchard.

New York Republican Rep. Mike Lawler in NY-17 could be one of the rare Republicans who holds on to his seat thanks in part to his party's ad spending advantage in the closing stretch against Democrat Cait Conley.

Three of Iowa's four House races, IA-01, IA-02 and IA-03 are on the list of most expensive, in part because of the competitive governor and U.S. Senate races that are also on the ballot and driving up turnout.

Other races to watch: CO-08, PA-01, WA-03, WI-03

This year's Supreme Court decision rolling back part of the Voting Rights Act led to many Republican-led states redrawing their House maps to eliminate districts that favor Democrats in a typical election year – but this year is not typical.

Now, the political environment has deteriorated so much that seats like AL-02, held by Democrat Shomari Figures, OH-09 held by Democrat Marcy Kaptur and NC-01 held by Democrat Don Davis are all considered toss-ups at best and favoring the Democrats at worst for Republicans.

In Texas, Latino voters seem poised to swing back to Democratic candidates and could prevent Republican Eric Flores from flipping the seat held by Rep. Vicente Gonzalez in TX-34 or Tano Tijerina from unseating Democratic incumbent Henry Cuellar in TX-28.

Other races to watch: CA-22, FL-09, FL-14, TX-35

As results continue to be reported, one thing to watch is how far into conservative communities the increased support for Democrats can go.

One race that has received a surprising amount of ad attention is in Arkansas's 2nd district (AR-02), where incumbent Republican Rep. French Hill faces a well-known challenger in Democrat Chris Jones. By the time polls close, closer to $10 million will have been spent in the final months on the Little Rock-based seat that Trump carried by 15 points in 2024.

There are also many districts in states that redrew their maps where the Republican gerrymander could backfire, like Ohio. In both OH-10 held by Republican Mike Turner and OH-15 held by Republican Mike Carey, Trump won by closer to 10 points in 2024. They're also in a state with competitive governor and Senate races and stretch between the suburbs and exurbs of Dayton and Columbus with an electorate that could swing away from the GOP.

The margin in North Carolina's Senate race, where former Gov. Roy Cooper is expected to win against former Republican National Committee Chair Michael Whatley, could determine if races like NC-03 are in play as well too. Rep. Greg Murphy's district absorbed more Democratic-leaning voters after the latest map redraw.

Other races to watch: NC-09, NY-21, TN-09, WA-05

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.
