#5885. EVs Sales Are Rising And Another Gasmobile Bites The Dust

The automotive press is practically aflame with rumors that Ford is replacing its Ford Escape crossover SUV with an electric version, also to be named Escape. That has raised quite a ripple across the esoteric field of car-naming. Normally when a model retires, so does the name. The US auto industry is littered with has-beens, wanna-be’s, never-was, and just plain exhausted cars that retired along with their names. Hmmm….perhaps Ford is sending a not so subtle signal about the future of EV sales in the US…

By now, everyone knows that EV sales cratered last fall, after US President Donald Trump and his Republican majority in Congress engineered the “OBBA” tax bill to eliminate federal tax credits for EVs.

Still, EV sales did not actually stop in their tracks, let alone reverse course. A fresh wave of off-lease, used EVs helped keep the momentum up earlier this year, and some automakers (looking at you, Subaru) stayed on track with plans for new EVs in the US.

Plug-in hybrids also factored in as a sort of gateway drug, with Audi CEO Gernot Döllner among those looking forward to strong EV sales in that category.

EV stakeholders must smell blood in the water as Election Day 2026 approaches. With polls indicating that  Trump will lose his chokehold on federal EV policy, news about EVs has been tumbling through the Intertubes over the past several weeks. Among the examples, Slate Auto opened its first showroom in Chicago, and General Motors unveiled its gen-4 EV platform.

Somewhat bizarrely, the Trump administration also contributed to the news cycle with the launch of the new “BattMan” battery collaboration earlier this week. Under the umbrella of the US Department of Energy, BattMan hooks up federal laboratories with academic and private sector partners to push the envelope on next-gen batteries for transportation applications as well as stationary energy storage.

Of course, Tesla also weighed in with its “flying car” stunt, but that was just a stunt. Over in the world of street legal EVs, Ford has been working the publicity machine to gin up interest in the forthcoming Fathom all-electric pickup truck, which is on track for volume production early next year at an MSRP starting at the affordability benchmark of $28,350 with a built-in inverter that cuts the cost of bidirectional charging, too.

The Fathom illustrates how much blood, sweat, and tears go into naming a new car. It’s a mid-sized pickup like the popular Maverick, but Ford went off in a new direction with the Fathom, considering hundreds of options over a months-long naming journey before settling on a maritime-adjacent reference to deep understanding.

The Fathom will be the first EV to roll off Ford’s new “Universal EV” manufacturing platform, and the automaker has indicated that the next vehicle will be a compact SUV. The consensus has settled on something resembling the Escape crossover SUV, all the more so because Ford has been laying plans to retire the gasmobile…but maybe, not the name.

That’s where the ears of the automotive press have pricked up, drawing attention to the Escape landing page that depicts the rear end of a forlorn, rain-splattered Escape heading off into the dustbin of automotive history along with its dual exhaust system.

And, here’s where things get interesting. The text on the landing page simply notes that the Ford Escape is “tapping the brakes,” which has been interpreted as a pausing message, not a forever message.

However, on the same page Ford provides a good reason why the 2026 Escape will not fly. The car is not for sale in California and several other states.

“The 2026 Ford Escape® is not certified for emissions requirements with the California Air Resources Board,” Ford states in a drop-down link. “Therefore, it is not available for purchase by customers intending to register their vehicle in states that have adopted California’s certification requirements for the 2026 model year, specifically California, Massachusetts, Oregon, New York, Washington, and Vermont.”

Ford goes on to suggest two other gas-powered SUVs that are street legal in California-compliant states, the 2026 Bronco Sport SUV and the 2026 Ford Explorer SUV.

So, is Ford really banking on the Escape brand to boost its EV sales in the US? Maybe! In August, the Detroit Free Press summarized a swirl of rumors around the idea of an electric Escape, citing insider sources including a Ford dealer with access to the company’s program codes.

However, in the same report, Reuters cites an inside source who indicated that in 2029, Ford plans to introduce a new entry-level SUV in hybrid and gasoline powertrains.

“At the Las Vegas meeting, the executives, the dealer said, assured dealers that the automaker is committed to hybrid powertrains going forward,” Reuters emphasized.

Hmmmm…so much for an all-electric escape. Back in May, the Canadian Ford dealer MSAFord also raised some questions about an all-electric replacement for the Escape in a blog post (breaks added for readabilty):

“Production of the Ford Escape and Lincoln Corsair ended at the Louisville Assembly plant last December, leaving a giant hole in The Blue Oval’s lineup as it pertains to small crossovers.”

“This is particularly true of the Escape, which disappeared at a time when new vehicle shoppers are increasingly looking for more affordable options, too.

“However, back in December 2022, a report surfaced from AutoForecast Solutions, claiming that the Ford Escape would live on as an all-electric crossover, though it has thus far remained unclear if that plan is intact ever since”

Readers, what do you think? Is half an EV better than none? Full EV sales have yet to surge, but plug-in hybrids have become more popular on the heels of US President Donald Trump’s witless decision to bomb Iran last February.

EREV (extended range EVs) are another halfway-there option that will factor into an uptick in EV sales. The Scout Motors branch of Volkswagen, for example, has stated that EREVs account for the majority of its reservations so far.

Then there’s the pure EV players, including robotaxi stakeholders alongside Slate and Rivian, which is forging ahead with plans to construct a new factory in Georgia.

As for industry leader Tesla, that’s a whole ‘nother can of worms. CEO Elon Musk has earned a fair share of bad publicity in recent years, as depicted in the new “Musk” documentary. However, the film wrapped sometime last year, leaving Musk’s latest hi-jinks for the sequel.

#5882. Electric Hydrofoils Save Energy, But Do They Earn Their Price?

Hydrofoils have an obvious appeal. Lift a boat’s hull clear of the water, reduce drag and move passengers faster while consuming less energy. I’ve written before about the proposed electric hydrofoil ferry between Vancouver, Bowen Island and Gibsons, where floating logs, coastal conditions and uncertain passenger demand make the economics difficult. Hydrofoils have been commercially available for decades, but their operating niche remains narrower than their technical appeal suggests.

A different opportunity emerged when Antwerp pilotage operator Brabo ordered an Artemis EF-12 electric hydrofoil pilot boat in August 2024. The vessel combines electric propulsion with submerged foils and active controls. Artemis has also demonstrated ship-contact manoeuvres using its EF-12 workboat platform, while the EF-12 Pilot design entered sea trials in 2026. The handling benefits are relevant to pilot transfers even when the boat isn’t operating at full speed.

The financial question is whether a hydrofoil can spend enough of its working life taking advantage of its design. Antwerp pilot boats work around locks, docks, ship movements and changing assignments. Reaching a high maximum speed is of limited value when the vessel spends much of its day manoeuvring or travelling through constrained waterways.

I tested that question using a Python-based operating model with synthetic assignments and three hypothetical sets of port-speed permissions. Depending on how much suitable high-speed water was available, the EF-12 spent zero, approximately 6% or approximately 13% of its underway time foilborne. These are modeled scenarios, not measurements of Brabo’s operations or actual Antwerp speed authorizations.

The resulting financial comparison was unflattering to the hydrofoil. In the central case, the EF-12’s modeled fifteen-year discounted cost was approximately €5.32 million, compared with €3.81 million for a conventional diesel tender, a premium of about 40%. Both figures include purchasing new vessels, rather than comparing a new hydrofoil with the marginal cost of keeping an existing diesel boat.

Electric propulsion still brought substantial energy benefits. The hydrofoil’s problem was that lower purchased energy did not compensate for the assumed capital cost and the limited opportunity to operate in its most efficient regime. Its active controls and potential ride-quality benefits remain relevant, but the available evidence did not justify assigning them a financial value.

An alternative electric approach also deserves consideration. Norway’s Moen Marin and Pascal Technologies have developed an air-cavity electric personnel vessel that reduces hull resistance without lifting most of the vessel above the water. It performed better financially than the hydrofoil in the exploratory comparison, although the existing craft was designed for aquaculture work and would require appropriate adaptation and certification before serving as a pilot tender.

The lesson resembles the one I drew from the Vancouver hydrofoil ferry proposal. Hydrofoils can be excellent machines, especially where sustained high-speed passages and suitable sea conditions allow them to exploit their advantages. In congested harbours, their additional complexity and capital cost need much stronger justification.

Different routes, operating permissions and vessel prices could produce different results. For commercial fleet decisions, the relevant benchmark is the work the vessel actually completes and the total cost of providing that service.

Michael Barnard has 1539 posts and counting. See all posts by Michael Barnard

#5881. DTE utility program funded a 132-MW Michigan solar project

DTE Energy’s 132-MW Fish Creek Solar Park near Sheridan, Michigan, is now operational. The $268 million development was fully funded by customers who voluntarily participate in DTE’s MIGreenPower program. The solar park can generate enough energy to power approximately 30,000 homes.

“Our customers expect us to keep the lights on and keep bills as affordable as possible, and bringing new generation like Fish Creek online is part of how we deliver on that commitment,” said Matt Paul, president and COO of DTE Electric. “Fish Creek adds more Michigan-made energy to our system while supporting local jobs, generating new tax revenue and delivering lasting benefits for the communities that helped make this project possible.”

Fish Creek will generate tax revenue, supporting regional infrastructure, such as schools, first responders, road maintenance and veteran and senior citizen services. The solar park also created hundreds of local jobs during construction and will provide participating landowners with supplemental income.

In addition, Bushnell and Evergreen townships each received $330,000 through Michigan’s Renewables Ready Communities Award, a grant available to local governments that host large-scale renewable energy projects. The townships plan to invest the funding in town hall and fire station renovations, along with other community improvements.

Fish Creek is part of DTE’s broader plan to meet Michigan’s growing energy needs with a mix of resources. DTE currently has 20 wind parks and 37 solar parks across Michigan, which together generate enough clean energy to power nearly 1 million homes. DTE’s 2026 Integrated Resource Plan includes an additional 15 GW of renewable energy by 2042.

#5879. Fuel From Bamboo Costs Just $2.75 Per Gallon

At CleanTechnica, we are passionate champions of the “electrify everything” movement promoted by Tony Seba and Mark Jacobson, among others. However, being realistic, internal combustion engines will continue to play an important role in transportation by land, sea, and air for the remainder of this century and perhaps beyond.

Almost any plant that grows could potentially be turned into a biofuel with a lower emissions footprint than gasoline or diesel. Currently in the US, millions of tons of corn are used every year to make ethanol, which is then blended with gasoline. In theory, the ethanol helps lower emissions, but the reduction, if any, is modest and it decreases fuel economy, so motorist are actually using more fuel than they would if they used ordinary, unadulterated gasoline.

The other part of the equation is that the conversion process itself requires a fair amount of energy and makes its own greenhouse gas emissions, which politicians conveniently choose to ignore. Ethanol, then, is mostly a scam that rewards farmers, but has few other benefits. While the science is sound, the rewards are far less than claimed. However, farmers are an important political group and no one wants to tell them that corn for ethanol is no longer a thing.

Researchers at Worcester Polytechnic Institute think they have found a better way. In a study published September 24, 2026 in the Royal Society of Chemistry journal Sustainable Energy And Fuels, Michael Timko, the head of the chemical engineering department at WPI, and  Timothy Woodard, PhD, claim that bamboo may be the perfect source material for making biofuels. In the published study, they write:

“Corn stover, an agricultural residue widely available in the U.S., has been one of the most studied biomass sources for biofuel production.6 Yet its relatively modest yields and high ash content have created significant challenges for scaling up production. Alternative feedstocks such as switch grass have been explored, but these offer only incremental improvements in performance relative to corn stover, particularly in ash content and growth rate. These limitations highlight the importance of identifying and evaluating less conventional biomass sources that may offer superior attributes for fuel production.

“Bamboo has emerged as a strong candidate due to its rapid growth, high productivity per unit area, and low input requirements. Some bamboo species reach their full height within a few months and mature within four years. Bamboo’s deep root systems improve soil stability, promote subsurface carbon sequestration, and support water conservation. It can also be grown on marginal or degraded land, reducing competition with food crops and offering economic opportunities for rural communities.

“Critically, bamboo also possesses an exceptionally low ash content compared with most lignocellulosic feedstocks, which minimizes inorganic fouling and catalyst poisoning during thermochemical conversion and improves the quality of the resulting biocrude. Furthermore, the presence of ash complicates thermochemical conversion to products, with frequently reported adverse effects on yields.

“The characteristics of fast growth, low fertilizer and water use, and low ash content make bamboo stand out as a promising source of biomass that can achieve economic competitiveness that has not been obtained with other feedstocks. Unfortunately, despite these advantages, bamboo remains largely underexplored as a feedstock for liquid fuel production, especially in thermochemical conversion processes such as hydrothermal liquefaction (HTL).”

Here’s the best part. The researchers believe biofuel from bamboo could be commercially viable at a price of $2.75 a gallon. That’s less than gasoline at the moment and less than half the cost of diesel. What’s more, when compared to diesel, the resulting emissions are up to 26 times lower. So let’s see:  Less than half the cost and only 5 percent of the emissions? Think anyone would take that deal? Oh, you betcha!

Bamboo is a grass, and one of the fastest-growing plants on Earth. Some species grow several dozen inches in a day. Because of this fast growth, the US could make as much fuel from bamboo as it makes ethanol from corn while using only 13 percent as much land.

“I think this research is especially timely and important because fuel prices are high and the transportation sector produces more greenhouse gas emissions in the U.S. than any other,” said professor Timko in a press release. “Bamboo would use different land than we are using for food production, so there is no food versus fuel trade-off. Farmers win, consumers win.

Using bamboo for fuel can reduce greenhouse gasses in two ways, the researchers maintain. First, the process to convert it into biocrude is more efficient than it is for other feedstocks. Second, bamboo itself captures and stores large amounts of carbon both above and below ground.

Unlike annual crops used for many biofuels, bamboo is a perennial grass that can produce large amounts of biomass with relatively low agricultural inputs. Compared with other perennial grasses, bamboo has very low ash content (less than 1% by dry weight), meaning that the conversion process generates negligible waste.

The economic modeling conducted by the researchers indicates that bamboo-based fuel could sell for as little as $2.75 per gasoline equivalent under mature commercial-scale conditions, accounting for the money required to build and operate the fuel plant, suggesting strong economic competitiveness as the renewable-fuel industry expands. Considering the ability of bamboo to store large amounts of carbon in its underground rhizomes, producing fuel from bamboo would generate a greenhouse gas intensity of 3.5 grams of carbon dioxide-equivalent per megajoule of energy, the researchers report.

“The U.S. has enormous land area under cultivation for corn ethanol, a fuel which is often blended into gasoline,” said professor Timko. “Freeing up at least some of that land could allow for more food production and reduce the burden on the environment. Bamboo may eventually offer industry a way to generate low carbon liquid fuels at scale. With further research and development, bamboo could be a compelling option for decarbonization in sectors that are difficult to electrify, including heavy duty transport and aviation fuel supply chains.”

The researchers also point out that bamboo can be cultivated on former industrial land or heavily farmed properties where other crops and vegetation struggle to grow. Their research found the growth rate of bamboo (22–44 tons per hectare per year) “is at least three times that of corn stover and at least twice that of switch grass.

Based on the lowest growth rate, an idealized circular [bamboo] plantation with a 30 mile radius corresponds to an estimated 16.1 million dry tons of biomass per year. Ash content, a critical parameter for processing efficiency and waste generation, was less than 1 percent in bamboo, compared with 4.8–7.3 percent for corn stover and 2.2–11.9 percent for switch grass.

With such data in mind, expect the current US administration to impose a heavy tax on bamboo based biofuel to protect the profits of the oil industry and corn growers. Nothing must be allowed to challenge the hegemony of fossil fuels or the profits of Big Ag. And please don’t tell the head doofus that the WPI research was funded in part by Pacific Northwest National Laboratory and the National Science Foundation. If he found that out, his head might explode!

#5878. Electric Vehicle Council Celebrating 10 Years of EV Progress in Australia

The Electric Vehicle Council has been tracking the progress of electric vehicle uptake in Australia over the last ten years. Their latest report is a celebration of achievements. It is 144 pages long, and you can read the entire report here. I will just be hitting the highlights and providing some comments along the way.

In a demonstration of how fast the EV transition is moving in Australia, the EVC report projections to the end of the year are already hopelessly out of date, with a surge over the last three months looking set to continue through to the end of the year. Nevertheless, the report makes encouraging reading.

Both BEV and PHEV sales have doubled comparing H1 2025 with H2 2026. A new EV is sold in Australia every 100 seconds as Australians seek to cut costs and gain energy security. The ratio of BEV to PHEV sits at 2:1, similar to many other countries. The report warns of a possible “fragility” in Australia’s EV sales due to increasing demand for public charging and the possible introduction of a road user charge.

The report says that so far installation of public charging has kept up with demand. This is due to the fact that most Australians charge at home. There are 4268 AC and DC public charging locations available, an increase of 18% year on year and 11,600 public AC and DC connectors, a 31% increase year on year.

Political threats might also derail the electric train of progress. Australia’s right wing political parties are threatening to remove the New Vehicle Efficiency Standards and the Fringe Benefit Tax exemption should they gain power. The battle is not yet won. Intense lobbying by vested interests in the auto industry (FCAI) has led to significant misrepresentation in the media. The FUD is ever with us.

The report notes that the FBT exemption has been reviewed and extended to 2029. This may be long enough for EVs to enter the second-hand market where most Australians buy their cars. Australia has set a target of 5 million BEVs on the road by 2035 — “uptake must keep accelerating” if the country is to achieve this target. At the time of this report’s publication, there were 612,000 BEVs on Australian roads.

The top 5 best-selling plugin EVs in the Australian market over the past 12 months have been: Tesla Model Y (20,400), BYD Sealion 7 (12,500), BYD Shark 6 PHEV (9,500), Geely EX 5 (6,800), and Jaecoo J5 (6,000). I don’t expect this to change as we rush towards the end of the year. CleanTechnica readers would have been familiar with this lineup from regular reports. For the latest one, see here.

A comparison with CleanTechnica’s figures and those of the EVC will demonstrate how rapidly sales are increasing. YTD figures of each model listed in the CleanTechnica report are almost double that in the EVC report.

There is quite the disparity between the takeup of vehicle classes, with buses leading the way. Thirty two percent of new buses sold in H1 2026 were battery electric, compared to passenger vehicles 26%, vans and trucks a miserable 1%. I am very surprised at this, as small business and its need for last-mile delivery would benefit from reducing their costs for transport. According to the report, there are 16 different vans and 30 trucks to choose from. Julie Delvecchio, the CEO of EVC, calls this “the single biggest unclaimed opportunity left.” Perhaps Australia needs some low-emissions zones in our cities? Sydney, Melbourne, and Brisbane would certainly benefit from this measure.

Utes make up a sizeable portion of the Australian market. At the moment, there are only 5 plugin utes available (mainly plugin hybrids like the BYD Shark 6). When this part of the market shifts to battery electric, I expect the game will be over for diesel.

The EVC supports a federal (as opposed to state by state) road user charge, which is appropriate and enables the benefits of EVs to be shared with all Australians. In other words, “a fair go.” As an EV driver, I am not opposed to an RUC in principle.

A series of videos about EV charging to educate and build confidence for the public have been produced by the EVC — you can get a taste here.

So far in 2026, ANCAP (Australasian New Car Assessment Program) has declared that 6 out 7 of the safest cars in Australia are EVs. Despite the squealing of legacy automakers about the NVES (New Vehicle Efficiency Standards), carmakers are easily meeting their emissions reduction targets. There is now serious consideration of EVs as a part of Australia’s energy system (V2G). We are moving beyond the early adopter phase and into the early majority — how soon we start the steep ascent up the “S” curve remains to be seen. Sales will certainly be helped by reduced prices of EVs. The BYD Atto 1 (aka Dolphin Mini, Seagull) is now the cheapest car of any drivetrain — less than AU$20,000.

The EVC report tells us that the same is now happening in the van segment. Electric vans are quickly approaching price parity with diesel. Again, events have overtaken the EVC report — three new vans have just launched with prices below AU$50,000, undercutting brands from Korea and Europe. This looks like the beginning of a price war in this sector. The absolute price leader is the LDV eDeliver 5 from SAIC. Then comes the Geely Farizon V7E. The best legacy automakers can offer is the Ford Transit City. The specs are very similar.

Comparing the eDeliver 5 to similar diesel configurations, it appears that the only advantage for diesel is double the range. For city runs, the electric vehicle is the best option. With fuel insecurity, increased prices at the pump, and lower maintenance, I expect there to be a surge in this sector. Year to date up till August, 46 eDeliver 5’s and 83 Farizon V7E have been sold.

Ten years have gone by since the first EVC report (I have been driving electric for 7 of those). Sometimes it feels like it has gone quickly, and sometimes too slowly for those participating in the rEVolution. The first EVC report cited an AEMO (Australian Energy Market Operator) prediction that 25% of new vehicles bought by Australians would be battery electric by 2035. We have already achieved that, 10 years early! To quote Chau Le, chair of the EVC, “If this is the transformation over the last 10 years, then the next decade will be nothing short of electrifying.”

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5877. HomePower 1000 Plus v2: Portable Power Put To Use

I have been using the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 in a wide range of applications over the past few months around my apartment in Queens, NY. The flexibility and portability make it especially useful for people living in apartments. I had previously written about the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 when it launched. You can read more about the technical specifications in that article.

Overall, the battery charged in about an hour. It starts charging at a little over 1000 W and then drops to a little over 800 watts as it charges. The first thing I plugged into the battery was a fan, which used about 50 watts. Then I plugged in my laptop, which charged at another 50 watts. I was able to work comfortably for a day off the battery with capacity to spare.

Keeping on the cool side, the battery was easily able to run my 12,000 BTU window air conditioner on a warmer day. Being in a century-old pre-war building, insulation could leave something to be desired. In addition, my corner apartment with large windows and 10-foot ceilings contributes to the load. Air conditioners can be a challenge to power, as the power consumption tends to spike when the compressor kicks in, but it did not pose a challenge to the battery. On a day in the mid-80’s (Fahrenheit), the battery was able to keep the indoor temperature in the mid-70’s all day with capacity to spare. Also, as my wife likes plugging her 1750 W clothes iron into the same circuit as the air conditioner, adding the battery could reduce the potential to trip the breaker when the compressor kicks in. It would be useful to maintain comfort during a power outage.

In terms of keeping things cool, the battery tends to use a little over 3% of its capacity per hour on my high-efficiency 14.1 cubic foot Midea refrigerator during the day with the door opening periodically. With the door staying shut, it would be closer to 2% per hour. That could potentially keep food from spoiling for a couple of days during an outage. While Jackery makes the FridgeGuard specifically for this application, the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 could come in handy for added capacity during an emergency.

The battery would also come in handy for people with fish tanks to keep the pumps running during a power outage. I have an aquaponics setup with a few small fish to grow herbs and start seeds in the Spring. The battery can easily run the filters, pumps and heaters, as well as the powerful lights for the plants. However, for someone with exotic fish in a saltwater aquarium, battery backup could be essential to keeping the fish alive during a power disruption.

In addition, I was able to run my Vitamix blender with the HomePower 1000 Plus v2. The powerful motor can draw over 1000 W, but it doesn’t take long to blend up a smoothie or frozen beverage. As such, you could easily make frozen margaritas at a backyard party using the battery to power the blender.

In terms of cooking, the battery could easily handle my rice cooker and Instapot pressure cooker for extended cooking times. I was able to cook an 8 lb. pork roast in the pressure cooker. It also had enough power to run my air fryer and microwave. However, with the air fryer drawing as much as 1700 W, it depletes the capacity quickly, even though the load is within the 1800 W continuous rating of the battery. A kettle or coffee maker does not pose much of a challenge. You could also potentially run an electric grill, although I did not have the opportunity to try it yet. This could make a cookout more convenient, in addition to reducing risks associated with fire-based grills in dry areas.

After all that, I needed to clean up. I was able to run my dishwasher off the battery. Heating water is clearly one area that consumes a lot of energy, but I was able to run the dishwasher for a full cycle with room to spare. I was also able to run my portable washing machine off the battery. While the loads were not massive, the voltage fluctuates significantly while it is running. It did not create an issue for the battery. Of note, these are efficient NYC apartment-sized appliances. Many Europeans use similar-sized appliances, but middle-American McMansions tend to use more power-hungry models. Your mileage may vary.

Shifting to a work environment. I was easily able to charge my laptop, run the router, charge my phone, etc., with capacity to spare. You could easily keep an office running during a power outage. Or set up a remote office for the day, returning to charge at night.

I also used the battery to run my Bambu Labs X1-C 3D printer. Overall, the load is small enough to produce large prints that last hours using just the battery for power. However, when plugged in, the battery can ensure consistent power through multiple prints. I used a power strip to turn on and off the power to the battery on several occasions. There was not a flicker or even the slightest variance in the print layers. If you have ever had a power disruption during a multi-hour complicated print, the benefit is clear. Moving forward, I wouldn’t use the printer on a large print without the battery. In addition to serving as a high-capacity UPS, I was also able to time shift the electricity consumption of the printer.

Using the Jackery App, I was able to easily set up the battery to use a TOU utility plan. By entering the times and rates, it can even calculate the savings. In addition, you can program the battery to retain a set amount of capacity for backup, seamlessly switching to input power when that capacity level is reached.

For Con Edison small business TOU rates, peak electricity supply from 8 AM to 10 PM is $0.5443/kWh from June 1 to September 30 and $0.2680/kWh all other months. Off peak is just $0.0199. Of note, this does not include the separate service charges, delivery charges and taxes, which on my last utility bill worked out to be $0.3378/ kWh on top of the electricity supply charges. Electricity is expensive in NYC. However, many small businesses rent and do not have access to the panel to take advantage of the potential TOU savings from a larger battery at that location. Smaller batteries can be implemented at point of use, such as a workstation. As such, a battery like a HomePower 1000 Plus v2 at each workstation could save roughly $0.50 per day in the summer and $0.25 in the winter. Payback on the investment could come in a few years. If a business changed locations, they could easily take the batteries with them. However, the ability to provide UPS battery backup could also easily pay for itself quickly if work could continue uninterrupted, work in progress could be saved and sensitive equipment could be protected.

To take it a step further, the HomePower 1000 Plus v2 can directly take DC solar input. This does not feed back into the grid, but it could provide power for different uses. This could be useful when camping or even with a more permanent solar setup to power an appliance, workshop or small office. While it would not replace the capability of balcony solar, the ability to run appliances off small-scale solar that does not feed into the grid could provide some of the benefits while the balcony solar regulations are being figured out.

Overall, I found the battery to be more useful than I originally anticipated. I tended to look at the power rating of different devices and assumed that they would pull that for the entire duration and drain the battery quickly. However, many cycle on and off, making 1024 Wh of battery go much farther than I would have thought. In addition, Jackery’s power and capacity ratings seem to be conservative, letting me use it right up to the rated limits without issues. And the portability lets me conveniently try it in a wide range of applications. Living in an apartment with efficient appliances, it also fits many of my potential use cases. And it gives people living in apartments who do not have the ability to modify the panel many of the benefits of battery storage. However, if the HomePower 1000 Plus V2 turns out to be  not enough for your needs, Jackery also offers expansion batteries to increase the capacity, as well as offering larger capacity models. While travel conflicts did not give me the chance to try it out for camping yet, I hope to use it outdoors soon.

Of course, Jackery also offers much larger batteries. And they have some even smaller batteries coming. But that is for another article.

Jackery supplied the HomePower 1000 Plus v2 for review, but did not pay for the review. The thoughts here are my own.

He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

Job seekers hoping to launch or advance a career in clean energy can connect with employers and explore new opportunities at the RE+ Clean Energy Job Fair on Nov. 18, at the Las Vegas Convention Center. The job fair will run from 11 a.m. to 2 p.m. and attendees can connect with hiring companies. The fair is open to people of all skill levels.

We thank our partners and sponsors for their support in bringing this event to Las Vegas.”

In 2025, more than 37,000 people attended RE+, an event with more than 1,300 exhibitors from businesses in solar, energy storage, electric vehicles, grid technologies and other sectors. This year’s conference will take place November 16–19 at the Las Vegas Convention Center.

“WRISE is excited to co-host this important event with GRID again this year,” said Lauren Friedman, senior manager of programs and events at WRISE. “The continued success of the job fair year over year speaks to the lasting strength and enthusiasm for our industry. We’re glad to make these opportunities available to the WRISE membership and our larger clean energy community.”

#5875. The Share of Solar Generation with Co-located Battery Storage Is Increasing

Utility-scale solar developers are increasingly co-locating battery storage with new solar photovoltaic capacity. As of September 2026, over 16% of new utility-scale solar capacity added this year is co-located with operable battery storage, compared with 2% in 2017, according to data reported to EIA by operators.

Reported plans for solar additions scheduled to come online in the last three months of this year show that the share with co-located battery storage could increase to 19% this year. Longer-term plans indicate that this trend will continue, with the portion increasing to more than 30% in plans submitted for 2027 and 2028.

When operators co-locate battery storage with solar, they can direct solar electricity output to the grid or to the battery, depending on the wholesale prices. Wholesale prices in regions with large solar participation tend to fall when solar contributions are high and rise as they decrease. Plants with both solar and battery storage can manage the combined output of their generating resources to maximize their value in a changing wholesale price environment.

We publish unit-level generator inventory data based on our annual and monthly data collections, including information on design attributes for all utility-scale plants. Although battery storage and solar units at the same plant often enter commercial service at different times, the EIA-860 and EIA-860M data collection program tracks these developments and provides a monthly summary that can be used to identify co-located technologies.

Southwest states have higher-than-average solar capacity shares relative to their total generating capacity and also have higher levels of co-located utility-scale solar and battery capacity. As of September, 13% of the total U.S. utility-scale generating capacity is solar photovoltaic, and 18% of that is co-located with battery storage. The share of solar generation co-located with battery capacity in California, Arizona, New Mexico, and Nevada is more than double the national average.

Principal contributors: Glenn McGrath, Alex Mey

The EIA collects, analyzes, and disseminates independent and impartial energy information to promote sound policymaking, efficient markets, and public understanding of energy and its interaction with the economy and the environment.

#5874. Toyota & Waymo Advance Autonomous Driving Collaboration for Safer Streets and Freedom of Movement for All

Toyota Motor Corporation (“Toyota”) and Waymo today announced the next phase of their autonomous driving collaboration, in pursuit of a world with safer streets and freedom of movement for all.

Building on the preliminary agreement announced in April 2025, Toyota and Waymo will begin initial operations to test and validate an autonomous Toyota bZ4X fleet in Japan in 2027, with deployment to the United Kingdom and other Waymo-serviced markets planned for the following years. This partnership marks a major milestone in the expansion of Japan’s first fully autonomous ride-hailing service using high-volume, domestically manufactured vehicles.

Toyota will build the vehicles at its Takaoka plant in Toyota City, Aichi Prefecture, Japan. The entire process will take place under one roof, from assembling the base vehicle to integrating Waymo’s AV hardware.

By pairing Toyota’s global manufacturing and vehicle development expertise with Waymo’s fully autonomous technology, the companies will accelerate scaling safety-validated Level 4 autonomous vehicles globally — helping provide people with transit that reduces human-error accidents, and opening up freedom of movement for those who can’t easily get around.

Beyond safety, the custom-outfitted bZ4X is designed for comfort and greater accessibility. Generous rear legroom and features such as automatic doors, a power liftgate, and ambient lighting will provide seamless entry and exit for riders.

Toyota and Waymo will continue to explore the future of autonomous driving technology for personally owned Toyota vehicles. Alongside Woven by Toyota, they will also explore additional initiatives that support their shared goal of fewer road safety incidents, including how insights from Waymo’s real-world operations, data-driven intelligence, and technology could holistically improve safety for other road users.

“Toyota’s commitment to zero traffic accidents resonates deeply with us at Waymo, and we’re proud to work together toward safer roads for all,” said Dmitri Dolgov, co-CEO of Waymo. “In areas where Waymo operates, we’ve demonstrated that we are up to 20x safer than human drivers when it comes to serious crash injuries, and our partnership with Toyota will help us bring the safety and convenience of autonomous driving to more people around the world, including key international markets like Japan. We chose Toyota because of our respect for the legendary safety, reliability, and quality of their vehicles, and we look forward to deepening our collaboration as we scale our fully autonomous Waymo Driver technology globally.”

“We are deeply grateful that Waymo, which helped pioneer fully autonomous driving technology, has chosen Toyota as its partner,” said Hiroki Nakajima, member of the board of directors and executive vice president of Toyota. “Safety technology makes the biggest difference when it reaches as many people as possible. This collaboration will help scale that technology and bring it to customers in Japan and beyond through Waymo’s ride-hailing service, and in parallel, Toyota will continue to explore how integrating vehicles with traffic infrastructure can make those roads safer still. No single company can achieve the goal of zero traffic accidents alone, but with partners like Waymo, we will get there one step at a time.”

SEOUL/OFFENBACH — Hyundai Motor’s all-electric IONIQ 3 has been named one of seven finalists for the European Car of the Year 2027 award. Selected by an independent jury of automotive journalists from across Europe, the nomination places IONIQ 3 among the contenders for one of Europe’s most established automotive awards. The winner will be announced on 8 January 2027 at the Brussels Motor Show.

Designed in Europe and produced at Hyundai Motor Türkiye’s İzmit plant, IONIQ 3 has been developed specifically for European customers. The fully electric compact hatchback combines Hyundai’s distinctive ‘Art of Steel’ design language, a spacious interior and intuitive technology with a driving range of up to 497 km (WLTP) in the Long Range version. With a starting price below €30,000 across European markets, IONIQ 3 aims to make electric mobility accessible to a broader range of customers.

“Being named a finalist for European Car of the Year is important recognition for IONIQ 3 and for the work that has gone into its development. The response from customers has been very encouraging, with orders already exceeding our initial expectations. Its aerodynamic design contributes to driving efficiency, while its spacious interior offers the practicality of a larger vehicle despite its compact exterior dimensions. Intuitive technology supports everyday usability, and accessible pricing makes electric mobility available to a broader range of customers.

The European Car of the Year award is an independent automotive competition established in 1964. Its international jury consists of 65 automotive journalists from across Europe, who evaluate eligible new vehicles based on criteria including design, innovation, performance, value for money and market positioning.

Seven finalists are selected from the initial list of candidates. Following further evaluation and voting, the overall winner of the 2027 award will be announced on 8 January 2027 at the Brussels Motor Show.

#5864. Can Silicon Valley Build A Safe Class 8 Truck? Early EV Semi Fires Expose The Experience Gap

If you drove through the East Bay area of California on Tuesday night, you probably got caught in a nightmare. A Tesla Semi traveling down Interstate 880 in Hayward caught fire during the evening commute. There was no crash, no multi-vehicle pileup, and no obvious collision. The driver noticed something was wrong, pulled over near Highway 92, and got out unharmed.

Then the lithium-ion pack did what high-energy battery chemistry does when it gets pushed past its thermal limits… By the time crews finished foaming down the wreckage, all that remained of the tractor was its rear axles and a pile of ash.

The California Highway Patrol shut down both directions of the freeway out of concern over toxic chemical smoke. Northbound lanes eventually opened back up, but southbound lanes stayed closed for nearly ten hours while fire crews managed the blaze and let the pack burn itself out. When the sun came up, all that remained of the tractor was its rear axles and a pile of ash.

This is the second time in just over two years that a single Tesla Semi has shut down a major California highway for half a day. Back in August 2024, a Tesla-operated Semi drifted off Interstate 80 near Emigrant Gap, hit a tree, and caught fire. It took 50,000 gallons of water, air drops from Cal Fire, and a 15-hour closure to clear the road.

Whenever this happens, the anti-EV crowd immediately pounces and claims electric trucks are rolling time bombs. That’s a lie, as we’ve repeatedly explained here, but the knee-jerk reaction from tech enthusiasts, which claims this is just normal trucking business, is equally disconnected from reality.

Something deeper is going on here, and it highlights an important cultural divide between Silicon Valley and the century-old world of heavy-duty commercial trucking. Instead of trashing people and pointing fingers, let’s look at a deeper lesson the industry can learn from this.

First, let’s look at the numbers before anyone starts cherry-picking data.

According to the National Fire Protection Association, US fire departments respond to roughly 15,000 large truck fires every single year. That is about 40 diesel rigs catching fire every day across the country. Blown turbochargers spray oil on hot exhaust manifolds, wheel-end bearings seize at 70 mph, and dragging drum brakes ignite recap tires. Diesel trucks burn all the time, so fire is not a phenomenon unique to electric trucks.

The difference is how they burn. When a diesel tractor catches fire, the local volunteer fire department can usually knock it down with an engine tank and some foam in 45 minutes. A heavy tow truck drags the scorched chassis off the road, sweepers clear the debris, and traffic starts moving again before the morning rush hour is ruined.

When an 800-plus kilowatt-hour commercial battery pack enters thermal runaway, the playbook goes out the window. The cells generate their own oxygen as they break down, turning the battery into a self-sustaining furnace. First responders cannot just drown it and walk away. Their only choice is to pump tens of thousands of gallons of water on it for hours or stand back in defensive mode to keep it from spreading while half a county gets gridlocked. At best, all they can do is hopefully cool it down enough to get it off the road before it might catch fire again.

Then there is the sample size problem. There are roughly 4 million Class 8 commercial diesel trucks on American roads. By contrast, the active Tesla Semi fleet is estimated somewhere between 150 and a few hundred early-production and pilot tractors.

Mathematically speaking, you cannot calculate a clean failure rate on an N of two. A single flat tire that catches fire and spreads to a battery pack can swing the statistical safety record of a 100-truck pilot fleet by 50%. But you also cannot ignore it when two trucks in a tiny pilot fleet cause catastrophic, 10-to-15-hour interstate shutdowns.

So, instead of being lazy and relying on just the numbers, we need to look for more context.

This is not uniquely a Tesla problem. It is an electric startup problem.

Remember Nikola? In the summer of 2023, several of their Tre battery-electric trucks caught fire, including one at their Phoenix headquarters and another in customer testing. Nikola initially hinted at foul play, but third-party investigators quickly found the real culprit: a coolant leak inside the battery packs supplied by Romeo Power had caused an internal electrical short. Nikola had to issue a voluntary recall on all 209 trucks they had built, halting sales and dragging every pack back to the shop to fix the design.

Startups approach trucking like consumer electronics. They design from a clean sheet, prioritize maximum aerodynamic efficiency, integrate components into custom monolithic structures, and move fast.

But, when you take the “move fast and break things” approach, the stakes are a lot higher in the trucking world.

Class 8 trucking is a brutal environment that eats clever ideas for breakfast. A big rig does not live the gentle life of a passenger car in sunny California. It flexes under 80,000 pounds of gross weight, takes violent torsional twisting over uneven loading docks, endures constant low-frequency road battering, and rattles across millions of frost heaves.

If there is a slightly loose coolant quick-connect, an undersized wire harness, or a busbar that flexes half a millimeter under torsional load, commercial duty cycles are like Liam Neeson in Taken. If you’re a problem of any kind, the duty cycle will find you, and it will kill you.

This brings us to the traditional truck builders. Why aren’t we seeing Freightliner eCascadias or Volvo VNR Electrics burning down to the rims on I-80? They’re electric, too, right?

It’s not because traditional truckmakers possess magical, fireproof battery chemistry. They buy lithium-ion cells from the same tier-one suppliers as everyone else. In fact, if you dig into public regulatory filings, other manufacturers have run into the exact same terrifying failure modes that bit Nikola.

In 2023, Volvo and Mack filed safety recall reports with the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) covering hundreds of electric trucks. What was the defect? Their battery supplier had two separate manufacturing errors: coolant lines that were not fully locked into place, creating the risk of conductive fluid leaking across cells, and over-torqued internal fasteners that could snap off and rattle around inside the pack as loose metallic debris. Both defects created direct short-circuit and fire risks.

Why didn’t you read about those trucks burning down on the highway? Because the so-called legacy manufacturers have spent a century building institutional safety systems.

They caught this problem when a small fire actually broke out in a battery pack in the factory. The fire stayed contained within the pack, but Volvo didn’t try to hide it or explain it away. Instead, they did what the old school companies do, even if it slowed them down. Within a week, there was a stop-ship order on the packs and trucks already on the road were called in for a swap.

Traditional truckmakers move at a glacial pace compared to Silicon Valley, and tech fans love to criticize them for it. But, that slow pace comes from decades of scar tissue earned in battle. When an old school builder sees an internal assembly error or gets a strange sensor reading in fleet monitoring, their reflex is not to push an over-the-air firmware patch and hope for the best. They trigger stop-ship orders, use their huge dealer networks to ground the fleet, file Part 573 defect notices with the feds, and tear the battery packs open in a service bay.

Their systems caught the defect in the shop before it had the chance to catch fire on the freeway.

There is also an important philosophical divide in how these trucks are packaged.

Tesla chose the moonshot approach. To hit a 500-mile range target while hauling full freight, they needed maximum energy density and minimal tare weight. That led them to build a massive, integrated, structural battery pack that sits low between the frame rails. It is an engineering marvel in packaging efficiency, but it is also a monolithic block of energy. If one section suffers an internal failure, the entire pack is at risk.

Traditional builders like Volvo and Freightliner (Daimler Truck) took a far more conservative, regional-haul route. Instead of packing nearly a megawatt-hour of energy into a single tightly integrated structural assembly under the cab, they use smaller, lower-capacity modular packs—with Volvo literally hanging separate battery enclosures off the outside of the frame rails where diesel saddle tanks normally sit.

Hanging heavy battery boxes on the side of the chassis is terrible for maximum range and adds unnecessary weight, and may leave them more vulnerable to collisions. But, from a safety and service standpoint, it is brilliant. The packs are physically isolated from each other. If one module suffers a mechanical puncture, a coolant leak, or a thermal event, the damage is far easier to contain, inspect, and unbolt.

Silicon Valley brought the heavy trucking industry kicking and screaming into the electric era. Without Tesla pushing the envelope on megawatt charging and long-range electric haulers, diesel truckmakers would still be dragging their feet with compliance pilot programs, if even that.

Building an electric truck that can pull 82,000 pounds is not just a software problem, and it’s not just a battery chemistry problem. It is a mechanical durability problem that has to survive millions of miles of rough roads, rough drivers, and unpredictable operating conditions.

Experienced truck builders know how to build a chassis that survives that abuse, but they were slow to embrace the battery. Silicon Valley knows how to build the battery, but they are still learning how punishing the road really is.

Until those two worlds finally learn from each other, fire crews are going to keep having very long nights on the interstate. Hopefully more cross-pollination and experience eventually lead to improvements for all sides of the industry.

Jennifer Sensiba is a long time efficient vehicle enthusiast, writer, and photographer. She grew up around a transmission shop, and has been experimenting with vehicle efficiency since she was 16 and drove a Pontiac Fiero.

#5861. Antwerp’s Electric Cranes Can Handle More Than Today’s Cargo

Katoen Natie has put two all-electric mobile harbour cranes into service at the Port of Antwerp, with four more joining them across three deep-sea multipurpose terminals. The completed fleet will include four 200-tonne cranes and two 150-tonne machines, with paired lifting capacity reaching 400 tonnes. Katoen Natie expects the six cranes to displace as much as 500,000 litres of diesel annually once they’re operating.

The October commissioning announcement is worth attention because these are heavy, mobile machines working in a commercial port. They aren’t small demonstrations, and the engineering addresses the conditions that historically made mobile harbour cranes difficult to electrify.

Fixed container cranes were straightforward candidates for electrification. They stay in one location, have repetitive operating patterns and can be connected to substantial electrical supplies. Mobile harbour cranes travel along the quay, move between assignments and handle cargo that can vary considerably from one vessel to the next. An onboard diesel generator provided that mobility without requiring the terminal to build electrical connections everywhere the crane might work.

Konecranes’ solution separates the energy needed for lifting from the energy needed for travel. External grid electricity supplies the heavy working loads, while rechargeable batteries allow the cranes to move between operating positions. This avoids carrying an enormous battery for work that can be powered directly from the grid. It also eliminates diesel exhaust at the equipment and reduces local noise.

The bigger advantage is that the investment isn’t tied to one type of cargo. A mobile harbour crane can handle steel, industrial equipment, large transformers, wind components, scrap, project cargo, conventional bulk and containers. Port operators can change their handling arrangements as customers and cargo flows change without replacing the basic lifting machinery.

That matters as the energy transition changes maritime trade. In an earlier CleanTechnica analysis, I examined how ports face rising capital requirements even as fossil-fuel cargoes begin their long-term decline. Coal, oil and gas have supported major investments in berths, terminals, channels and inland connections. Their declining role creates commercial risks for infrastructure designed around individual commodities.

Ports are looking for new business in containers, renewable-energy equipment, industrial products and alternative fuels. Some will succeed, but they cannot all capture the same growth in container traffic, and low-carbon molecules will not replace fossil-fuel cargo tonne for tonne. Equipment investments have to make sense under more than one plausible freight forecast.

Electric mobile cranes are well suited to that uncertainty. A machine capable of lifting a transformer today can handle scrap steel or construction machinery tomorrow. Its electrical supply can also be part of a wider investment supporting yard equipment, trucks, shore power and harbour craft.

That doesn’t make every new crane or grid connection worthwhile. A terminal still needs sufficient durable activity, suitable electrical infrastructure and a sound operating case. But investing in flexible electric equipment is less dependent on predicting which replacement cargo wins than building another dedicated fossil-fuel or specialized alternative-fuel facility.

Katoen Natie’s investment demonstrates how ports can reduce diesel consumption today while retaining more options for the freight they handle tomorrow.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing article connects Antwerp’s electric mobile harbour cranes with the larger challenge of investing in ports as fossil bulk declines, new industrial cargoes emerge and electrical infrastructure serves more of the waterfront.

#5855. Elon Musk’s Attack Ads Are Backfiring As Midterms Near

US Federal Election Commission data revealed this week that Elon Musk has donated at least $445 million into Republican-aligned political action committees and groups since last year. Interestingly, it seems that Musk’s deep influence isn’t enough, and he is worried.

If Democrats win big on Tuesday in midterm elections, Musk insists US democracy will come to an “end.”

“This would result in America becoming a permanent one-party state and the end of democracy in any meaningful sense of the word,” Musk wrote Friday on X. “All that is needed to ensure this doesn’t happen is 51 Republican senators in 2029.”

The Tesla and SpaceX CEO has a long track record of engaging in political quid pro quo. His companies receive billions in federal contracts. The once climate-is-all advocate now weighs heavily on the side of oil/fossil fuels. His DOGE actions may have forced labor shortages in regulatory agencies that were scrutinizing his businesses.

But now Musk seems distraught that newfound Democratic Congressional power could make him a target of investigations after the midterms.

And his fears may have merit. Musk’s blatant attempts to influence election results are getting a lot of recent analysis and condemnation.

“I worry about what Musk’s impulsive bullying is doing and has done to others more vulnerable than I am,” former Secretary of Labor Robert Reich wrote earlier this month after Musk exclaimed, “Prison for Reich.” Such extreme comments are part of Musk lifelong pattern, Reich continued, in which “he’s threatened and racially denigrated and pushed around (people) his whole life.”

Reich added that he is worried for that voters whom Musk has been “deluging with TV ads for Republican candidates” as well as for “federal workers who have been hurt by his so-called Department of Government Efficiency, Americans who have been harmed by his wanton destruction of public services, and people around the globe who are worse off because of cuts to USAID pushed by Elon’s DOGE.”

Musk’s America PAC has contributed to 43 US House and Senate races, and that influx of cash has funded vast amounts of television commercials, digital ads, text messages, and mailers. It’s almost easy to forget that Musk became a persona non grata with Trump and the Republican Party last year.

America PAC stands for “secure borders, safe cities, free speech, sensible spending, fair justice system, and self-protection,” according to its website.

These objectives might sound fair and reasonable to many voters, yet a majority of America PAC money goes to attack ads. Musk typically cross-posts America PAC’s damaging content to his more than 200 million followers on X, the social media site he owns and whose social commentary he controls.

Ezra Klein, co-author of Abundance, recently interviewed Quinn Slobodian, professor of international history at Boston University. Slobodian interprets Musk’s midterm campaign finance moves as “his flexing of his own power to make or break candidates, as he wishes,” which, in turn, is a way “fine-tune that control over the media and messaging ecosystem and get out ahead of what he worries would be a regulatory wave that would accompany a Democratic majority.”

Why are such media messages — even those that are based on false premises — so compelling during the run-up to the 2026 US midterm elections? Voters frequently base their own political decisions on fake information and images rather than on reality. Social historian Heather Cox Richardson explains that many such techniques draw scenarios of a chaotic society and destroy individuals’ “ability to make sense of the world.” Some media consumers “turn to a strongman who promises to create order.” Others will get so exhausted they simply refuse to fight back. The path of least resistance is succumbing to the manipulation and “giving up control of their government to an authoritarian.”

In Texas alone, America PAC has provided more than $33 million toward the Ken Paxton Senate campaign. Additionally, Musk donated $168 million contribution to the Senate Leadership Fund, which routes money back to the Paxton campaign.

“Elon Musk wants our land. He wants our water. He wants our tax dollars,” James Talarico, the Texas Democratic nominee for Senate, said at a recent rally. “He wants his very own personal United States senator.” Talarico’s comments were supported by loud boos from the crowd.

At a rally at the University of Texas, Talarico said Musk was trying to “stop the movement that we are building in our state.” In contrast, he offered that his own campaign is “funded by 1.3 million individuals who have donated their hard-earned money.” Instead of corporate PAC money, Talarico has depended on small-dollar donations.

The day before that, Talarico’s campaign released a digital advertisement accusing Paxton of being a mere pawn to Musk. “Let’s remind Elon: Texas is not for sale.”

Interestingly, Democratic candidates in Texas and elsewhere across the US are holding their own — many electoral races are too close to call, even with Musk’s influence and campaign finance imbalances helping out Republicans.

Carolyn Fortuna, PhD, is a writer, researcher, and educator with a lifelong dedication to ecojustice. Carolyn has won awards from the Anti-Defamation League, The International Literacy Association, and The Leavey Foundation. Carolyn owns a 2022 Tesla Model Y as well as a 2017 Chevy Bolt. Please follow Carolyn on Substack: https://carolynfortuna.substack.com/.
